--- Page 35 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the mystical significance of the "Eighteen Benedictions" (Amidah) prayer and the ritual of stepping backward to depart from the Divine Presence. This page shifts focus to the internal state of the worshiper, contrasting the superficial joy of the masses with the true spiritual joy of the righteous when performing religious commandments.</meta>

The common people observe the <term>Tzaddikim</term> <note>original: *tzaddikim* — refers to exceptionally righteous or saintly individuals in Jewish tradition</note> and see that they sit quietly, in prayer and in joy. Likewise, one also sees among the simple common people that they dance and are happy—so the simple person reasons:

## 22

<image-desc>A large diagonal ink stroke crosses through this entire paragraph, from the upper left toward the center right.</image-desc>

That this is all the same—the joy and happiness he sees in the <term>Tzaddik</term> when he performs a <term>Mitzvah</term> <note>original: *mitzvah* — a religious commandment or good deed</note>. He errs in this and considers himself also to be a righteous person, because he experiences that same joy during the performance of the commandment. But this is a mistake. For the joy which the righteous person has is a spiritual joy <note>original: *ruchaniyut*</note>, and it comes from the soul, which rejoices in the holiness of the commandment. However, the simple person who has that same joy—his joy is material <note>original: *gashmiyut*</note>, because he rejoices in the physical act he is doing, yet he thinks to himself that he is also a righteous person. In this, he is mistaken. Therefore, the verse tells us: "Praiseworthy is the man who fears the Lord" <note>original: *Ashrei ha-ish yerei et Hashem* — Psalm 112:1</note>. When he rejoices with the commandment, he should not calculate that he is equal to a righteous person. Rather, "in His commandments he desires greatly" <note>original: *be-mitzvotav chafetz me'od* — the second half of Psalm 112:1</note>—meaning he should desire the commandment itself. —

## 23

<image-desc>A large diagonal ink stroke crosses through this paragraph as well, mirroring the one above it.</image-desc>

We can further explain the verse "in His commandments he desires greatly." A person should not calculate when he performs a commandment that he should desire the commandment for the sake of a reward. Or, that he performs a commandment so that people should grant him importance and honor him. That is not the correct joy which a person ought to have. Rather, "in His commandments"—meaning he should desire only the commandment itself, not for the sake of other things. And when a person reaches this level—that he performs a commandment "for nothing," not for any honor and not for any reward—then he can explain the holiness of the commandment and shall rejoice with a whole heart that he was privileged to fulfill the will of the Creator. And this is a thing that is not difficult for any person to do, if he will <unclear>reflect deeply</unclear> and see that all of this world is absolutely nothing, and that only the commandment will remain with him forever. Then he will truly rejoice—

## 24

Regarding what the verse says, "in His commandments he desires greatly," it comes to teach a person that he should have a passion to do the commandment, and not merely as "a commandment of men learned by rote" <note>original: *mitzvot anashim melummadah* — a reference to Isaiah 29:13, describing mechanical or habitual religious practice</note>. He should not reach the level of "they have made the commandments a habit." Therefore, it says "desires greatly"—that he should have desire for every commandment he performs, as if he had never performed it before. He should have a new passion and a new joy every time, like one who does a thing for the very first time. Therefore, the verse says "in His commandments he desires greatly," so that his desire shall always be "great"—like something that is very dear to him and a source of delight—

<summary>This page distinguishes between superficial, physical joy and true spiritual joy in religious practice, warning against performing commandments for honor or out of habit. It emphasizes that true joy comes from desiring the "mitzvah" itself as a way to connect with the Creator, treating every act as if it were brand new.</summary>
<keywords>Tzaddik, Mitzvah, spiritual joy, material joy, Psalm 112, sincerity, rote practice, holiness, reward and honor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the previous discussion’s exploration of spiritual states but moves from individual joy into a complex mystical analysis of history, focusing on the "Merit of the Ancestors" and the spiritual "shadows" or "darkness" associated with specific years in the 17th century. It connects the 400 years of the Egyptian exile to contemporary spiritual struggles.</meta>

# 57

He said to him: "A son of four hundred years" <note>This refers to the 400 years of exile prophesied to Abraham in Genesis 15:13.</note>—God delivers through them. It is like Abraham who stood and selected fifteen over fifteen, etc., and He, may He be blessed, established him as the **Light of the World** and said, "Behold, the Chariot" <note>original: *Merkavah* — the Divine Throne-Chariot in Kabbalistic vision</note> is there, as it is written. And he established the **Merit of our Ancestors** <note>original: *Zechut Avot* — the spiritual credit earned by the Patriarchs that aids their descendants</note> as it is written, "And the Lord kept..." etc. Now, at this hour, it began in the second millennia, as if it were the **Crown** <note>original: *Keter* — the highest and most incomprehensible of the ten *Sefirot* or Divine emanations</note> which the world cannot endure at all. And so the Place <note>original: *HaMakom* — a traditional name for God, implying He is the "Place" of the world</note> measured and saw and knew the **darkness** <note>original: *shachar* — can mean both 'dawn' and 'darkness/blackness' (shachor); here it refers to a period of spiritual difficulty</note> of this year, which is according to the darkness of the year 5436 <note>original: *Pazlo* (פזל"ו) — the Hebrew year corresponding to 1675–1676 CE</note> and Lebanon. And behold, this aspect is drawn into the luminaries, etc. And those two generations of which it was said, "Two generations for **Understanding**" <note>original: *Binah* — the third Divine emanation, associated with the maternal and analytical</note>, because this light of **Sovereignty** <note>original: *Malchut* — the final emanation, representing the physical world and the Divine Presence in it</note> did not reveal all this until it reached the holy hand of B.N. <note>likely an abbreviation for a specific person or a mystical title, possibly 'Binah' or a contemporary leader</note> in which the darkness of the month of *Tammuz* <note>The fourth month of the Hebrew calendar, historically associated with the beginning of the destruction of the Temple</note> ceased. Its end turned away, and the sign "from there they shall fall" appears, as it is stated in the **Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note>.

And this is what is meant by "four hundred"—a tongue that points to the aspect of the **Year of Darkness**, where the debt of the "husks" <note>original: *kelipot* — the spiritual shells or forces of evil that conceal Divine light</note> and the "deviants" were mixed together, like the wilderness of Egypt which contained aspects of desolation.
<margin>For in truth there are three aspects at the **Gate** <note>original: *Sha'ar* — a common term in Kabbalah for chapters or spiritual levels</note></margin>
And so all the nations are built, or the Land of Canaan, which is mixed with **Kindness** and **Mercy**. And even though the leader in their matters are three mixed groups: <insert>Kindness, Power, Beauty; Victory, Splendor, Foundation</insert> <note>original: *Chag"at Nah"y* — acronyms for the six emotional Sefirot: *Chesed, Gevurah, Tiferet, Netzach, Hod, Yesod*</note> which in truth exist in the secret of the letters *Heh* and *Yod* <note>The letters of the Divine Name</note>, like the **Holy Seal** regarding the four generations whose sins—of these wicked ones, may God protect us—[caused this]. Look closely and you will find in our generation that the aforementioned **darkness** was not explained in any generation that preceded us; thus far.

And afterward, one learns that the aforementioned darkness is drawn from **Victory and Splendor** <note>original: *Netzach* and *Hod*</note> and upon "measures" from the matter of **Wisdom** and **Kindness**, five over five as mentioned above. For the power of the "bitterness" is the secret of the **Ancient Speech** that was presented and inherited. And he understood that it is necessary to repair [the world] from these three generations that came from Victory and Splendor, from the year to the **Holy Tongue** <note>original: *Lashon ha-Kodesh* — Hebrew</note>. And **Sovereignty** enters into the **Divine Presence** <note>original: *Shekhinah*</note> called by the letter *Vav*, the darkness of the **Living God**, the letter *Heh*, and it stands opposite the dawn of the sun. And these are his words: "The Merit of the Ancestors revealed, as it were, this abundance for the sake of every person." And this promise he said to those who despair, God forbid, that He heard the voice of the prayer of the bitter person, and especially through the labor of the **Torah**. And this is from the aforementioned holy manuscript from the year 5440 from the creation <note>1679–1680 CE</note>.

32 <note>original: ל"ב (Lamed-Bet) — refers to the 32 Paths of Wisdom in Kabbalah</note> Moses our Teacher, peace be upon him <note>original: ע"ה (Alav HaShalom) — a traditional blessing for the deceased</note>, leads us on the same path which <gloss>after another 49</gloss> <note>original: מ"ט (Mem-Tet) — refers to the 49 Gates of Understanding</note> can become different elsewhere, where we go through the path with him as our leader, and follow him and do not abandon him. <meta>This page transitions from the spiritual leadership of Moses into the practical, legal authority of community leaders. It anchors the mystical "blessing" of Moses mentioned on the previous page into the concrete reality of communal law, the binding nature of signatures, and the authority of majority rule within the Jewish community.</meta>

As it is said, "And of Dan he said, 'Behold'" <gloss>Behold</gloss> <note>original: ולדני אמר הן — a reference to Deuteronomy 33:22, continuing the theme of Moses' final blessings</note> they are a treasure, and He handed over to him all the portion which the Lord <gloss>may He be blessed</gloss> gave him, and I am here today according to their word. And he did not dispute them. And these are the acts that took place in the **Court of Mercy** <note>original: B.D. (Beth Din) shel Chesed — a Rabbinical court specifically noted for its focus on kindness or perhaps a specific local institution</note>, who would extend their authority into the synagogues and [duty] to stand against their priests, as was the ancient custom among the children of Israel, as well as the leadership of the community where they reside. They would enact **regulations** <term>Taqqanot: communal ordinances enacted by Jewish leaders to govern civil and religious life</term> and uphold them, and he arranged the law for them in the presence of everyone, which was written concerning them in their own handwriting, and they signed their names, each man for himself. And he also said that in our opinion, they should be judged according to everything they wrote about themselves, and they acted as sages over them on the condition, and included in this are the matters they stipulated with them; this is simple and clear. Ending: <gloss>Everything is valid and established</gloss> <note>original: Sharrir v'kayyam — the standard legal formula used to finalize Jewish contracts and documents</note>.

And regarding the law of the aforementioned community, who stood by a certain regulation and strengthened it upon themselves, and accepted it upon themselves in writing and signed in their presence, and made a regulation with the consent of the majority of the **community elders** <term>Tuviei Ha-Ir: literally "the good men of the city," referring to the elected lay leaders or the town council</term>. And the regulation made by this community <gloss>or</gloss> those written with one another; and it is known that there is a **ban** <gloss>the aforementioned</gloss> <term>Cherem: a formal communal shunning or excommunication used to enforce community standards</term> in the <unclear>exempted</unclear> <gloss>and signed</gloss> document. From such a regulation, one cannot retract at all. We found in the name of the **Rashba** <note>Rabbi Solomon ben Aderet (1235–1310), a preeminent Spanish legal authority whose responsa shaped Jewish communal law</note> in a legal response which the late Rabbi <gloss>may his memory be for a blessing in the world to come</gloss> cited elsewhere: that regarding a regulation made by the community in a gathering of all of them <gloss>or</gloss> even in the presence of individuals of the community, there is no power to retract them. Behold, it is clear to you from the words of the Rashba, of blessed memory, that a regulation made by the community by the majority of the community <gloss>and individuals of the community</gloss> cannot be changed except by a majority of the community. And how much more so in the presence of everyone; and if they accepted it upon themselves in the presence of everyone, there is no doubt that they relied upon it, and it stands firm against anyone who comes to challenge them. And above all else, that which they accepted upon themselves with their signatures in writing; for it is established for us that whoever accepts upon himself <gloss>or via a messenger</gloss> in a court a serious matter <gloss>or in any place</gloss>, etc. Meaning <gloss>and this is established for us</gloss>.

<meta>The text transitions here from the dense, theoretical Kabbalistic discourse of the previous page—focusing on the 42-letter Name of God—to a more accessible, communal preface. Many people have also asked me and requested that I compose some book in the language of Ashkenaz <note>original: "Leshon Ashkenaz"; here referring to Yiddish, the common spoken language of Jews in Central and Eastern Europe during this period</note> so that simple people, as well as women and children, might be able to read it and understand it well. Through this, they will recognize the Lord and know the fear of Him. This was done so that it may serve as a memorial for the final generation, and also for any person who wishes to perform repentance <note>Hebrew: "Teshuvah"; literally "returning," the process of atonement and spiritual realignment after committing a sin</note> for the sins they committed all their days, etc.

There are also good and excellent stories here to help one find favor in the eyes of God and man, by seeing the mercies of the Lord and His providence over us from the day the world was created until now, and the mercy of <gloss>the Omnipresent</gloss> <note>original: "Ha-Makom"; literally "The Place," a Rabbinic title for God emphasizing His presence everywhere</note> upon them, and how He saved us from every trouble and distress. For this reason, I have composed this account to show the love of the Holy One, Blessed be He, for His people Israel in every single generation, and the greatness of the Holy One, Blessed be He, and also that He is merciful and gracious and will not decree against us on the day of wrath <margin>etc.</margin>

Similarly, it is also found that our ancestors suffered great and strange troubles from their enemies, and despite all this, they kept their faith in the Lord and also sanctified His name in public <note>original: "Kiddush HaShem"; the act of bringing honor to God, often through martyrdom or ethical behavior in the face of persecution</note> with great joy and self-sacrifice. <gloss>And behold, these are</gloss> the stories that occurred in the world, and they were written by the famous and dear physician, Rabbi Joseph Ha-Kohen <note>Yosef Ha-Kohen (1496–1575), a famous Sephardic physician and historian who lived in Italy</note>. He called it by the name **The Vale of Tears** <note>original: "Emek Ha-Bakha"; a reference to Psalm 84:7, used here as the title of a famous history of Jewish suffering</note> <margin>etc.</margin> because it is full of troubles and tears, but also full of ethical instruction and righteousness. <gloss>It is from Heaven.</gloss>

With all this, the reader will see within it the mighty deeds of the Lord, His mercy, and His goodness which He has bestowed upon us from long ago <gloss>from day to day</gloss> and every single day. One will also see the individual providence that is constantly with us in this narrative, as is necessary to strengthen the hearts in the fortress of the Torah. Therefore, it is impossible to tell them all, for they are too numerous to count in writing. For this reason, <gloss>what was done briefly</gloss> is that this book before us was written for all people with extreme brevity. The account is sealed, and its name shall be called **The Vale of Tears**.

# The History of the Priest's Synagogue in Krakow

This is Isaac the Priest, son of Abba <note>Isaac Jakubowicz (died 1653), a wealthy banker and elder of the Krakow Jewish community</note>, of blessed memory, who built this synagogue here in **Krakow** in the year 5337 <note>1577 CE; though historical records usually date the construction of the Izaak Synagogue to the 1630s-40s, this manuscript records a local tradition</note>. With the help of God, may the memory of the righteous be a blessing.

And after him stood his son, the Rabbi Judah, of blessed memory, who was buried beside his father in the section of the righteous.

And it was in the days of the aforementioned Rabbi Judah that **Emperor Charles VI** <note>Holy Roman Emperor, reigned 1711–1740</note>, of blessed memory, came. And behold, there was a noblewoman here in Krakow, the wife of the pious and great Rabbi Simeon, son of Rabbi Mordechai **Segal** <note>original: "Segal," an abbreviation for *Segan Levi*, indicating a member of the Levite lineage</note> here. Her name was **Rachel**, of blessed memory; she was a woman who feared the Lord and was very great in her acts of charity and kindness. The leaders of the community here in Krakow designated a specific plot for her to be buried alone, and so they did, and they paved it with stones—

---

<margin>108</margin>
And it happened that the son of the aforementioned Rabbi Judah, whose name was Rabbi Zenwil, also passed away. The *Gabbai* <note>a synagogue administrator or treasurer</note> did not want to allow him to be buried next to his father because there was a separation <note>likely a space or another grave blocking the direct lineage path</note>. The sons of the late Rabbi Judah argued that they were his children and heirs, and that the merit of their father stood for them. In the end, their words were of no use; even the elderly Rabbi Isaac and the community leaders stood against them, and they were all of one mind.

---

<margin>109</margin>
<margin>Black</margin> And so it was, that one day a very great smoke came out from within the building, **black as a raven**. All the people found there saw it, and suddenly they saw the entire building fall to the ground. From the house, nothing remained except for one column standing over the place where the **Holy Ark** <note>original: "Ha-Kodesh Penimah," the inner sanctum</note> had been. There, the **menorah** <note>the seven-branched lampstand</note> remained lit. Suddenly, the smoke went forth, and from a distance, it appeared like a vision of a very great flaming fire. Within the smoke, there appeared the likeness of a man riding a **white horse**. He came to the entrance of the synagogue and appeared to the entire congregation.

He was the late Rabbi Isaac the Priest <note>original: "Katz," an abbreviation for *Kohen Tzedek* or Righteous Priest</note>, and he answered and said to them: "Do you not see and feel that this trouble has come because of the sin of harming the honor of my father, the Priest?" etc.

---

<margin>110</margin>
They sat and returned in complete repentance <note>original: "Teshuva"</note>. They granted him a place beside his father, and they buried both of them there, one next to the other. Until this very day, the synagogue stands, rebuilt as a very, very beautiful building, according to the good hand of the Lord upon us. They strengthened their hands with power to uphold the established tradition <note>original: "Chazakah," a legal right or ancestral claim</note>.

It happened with a certain very wealthy Jew who possessed a great deal of money. He came to the table and recited the **Grace After Meals** <term>Birkat HaMazon: The series of blessings recited after a meal containing bread</term> in a loud voice, and it was clear that his heart was directed toward heaven. <meta>The previous page concluded with a quote from Rabbi Judah the Pious regarding divine providence, the merit of ancestors, and a prayer for the restoration of the House of David during the exile. 36. It is known to every person that one is not obligated to perform the commandments of the Name <note>original: *HaShem*, a common way to refer to God, literally "The Name"</note>, blessed be He, for the sake of a total reward, and so on. Regarding this, it was said to him that for this reward he is hired for the service of the King, may He be blessed. For this reason, our Sages of blessed memory <term>Hebrew: *Hazal*, an acronym for the Jewish sages of antiquity</term> instituted these praises, such as [the prayers said] every day, and so on. It is fitting for a person to be happy with this portion, for it is a portion for the World to Come <note>original: *Olam HaBa*, the afterlife or the future messianic era</note>. For we find regarding one who ascended to the firmament and saw the face of the Divine Presence <term>Hebrew: *Shekhinah*, the indwelling presence of God on earth</term>, and so on; and it is written there that for all other people who perform the commandment with joy and pleasure, he [God] was not able to remove the world's suffering, and so on. Regarding this, it is said: **The Merciful One desires the heart** <note>original: *Rachmana liba ba'ei*, a famous Aramaic legal and ethical principle stating that God values sincere intention over mere mechanical performance</note>.

36. It is known that whoever merits this soul <note>the "Living Soul" mentioned later</note> merits the service of the Name, blessed be He, according to the value of the spiritual level within it. In reward for this commandment, he will merit to ascend to a soul higher than it. Behold, a God-fearing person who reminds his soul and purifies his heart to be fearful and trembling at the words of the Place <note>original: *HaMakom*, a name for God implying He is the "Place" or foundation of the world</note>, blessed be He—this commandment earns him a great reward. This is what is called **Service of the Heart**. For example, if he were sitting in the House of Study <term>Hebrew: *Beit Midrash*, a place dedicated to Torah study</term> and was meditating on the Torah and the commandments and finding pleasure in them—this is the essence of the service. And regarding this it is said: ->Serve the Lord with gladness<- <note>Psalm 100:2</note>. What is meant by "with gladness"? The general rule regarding the aforementioned joy is that the more joy a person adds to the commandment, the [greater] his service, and so on. We thus learn that according to the value of a person's intellectual level and the intellect that illuminates his soul, so shall his service be complete. This is called the "Complete Service," for as much as a person increases his intellect, there is a corresponding increase of joy in his heart, in understanding, and in the commandments, with the fear of the Name, blessed be He, always upon his face. And this is:

<margin>Side A</margin>
the essence of the service hinted at in this soul. For it is a **living soul** <note>original: *nefesh chaya*</note>; from it, one comes to the service of the Name, blessed be He, in love and joy. This is not the case with other souls, such as the **vegetative soul** <note>original: *nefesh tzomach*, the part of the soul responsible for growth and biological functions</note> and the **vital soul** <note>original: *nefesh chiyunit*, the animal soul or life force</note>, for these souls do not do the will of their Creator except through the necessity of the intellect. But this [living] soul, being intellectual, is always happy and delights

---

<margin>Side B</margin>
in spiritual pleasure and soulful delight. One says here: **"Who spreads the shelter of peace over us, and over all His people Israel, and over Jerusalem."** <note>original Hebrew: *Ha-pores sukkat shalom...* This is a central blessing from the *Hashkivenu* prayer, recited during the evening service, which takes on special significance on the Sabbath.</note>

Therefore, we say on Sabbath eve "Who spreads the shelter of peace," because the Sabbath is a **King of Peace** <note>original Aramaic: *Malka de-Shalom*, a mystical title for God used in the Zohar to describe the Sabbath as a time of divine harmony</note>, and it is holiness upon holiness, etc.; it is full of substance.
The King of Peace is the Kingdom, and the King of Peace is higher than the "King of the weekday." Therefore, one says on Sabbath eve: "Who spreads the shelter of peace."

This is to say, [there is] a greater likeness from this "garment" <note>In Kabbalah, spiritual lights are often described as being "clothed" in garments (*levushim*) to be accessible to human perception.</note> of the shelter of peace; the Sabbath is an **additional soul** <term>Hebrew: *Neshama Yeteira*; the Jewish tradition that a person receives an extra spiritual dimension or "soul" during the Sabbath to allow for deeper joy and rest</term> which comes from this.

One must also be <unclear>fearful</unclear> of it. Therefore, understand the verse regarding his trembling, and moreover, one should conduct the Sabbath with great awe.
One stands with all their strength so that they can, during the entire time, perceive it quite correctly and receive the "good memory" [from] their additional soul and can receive the "hidden world." One takes for themselves and becomes worthy of every bit of awe through a Sabbath,
greatly; and through this, one can receive goodness. Therefore, we say immediately:

...which the Lord gave to me, and in the days of my father-in-law <note>The Hebrew abbreviation א"מ stands for *Adoni Mori*, "my master and teacher," a common respectful way to refer to a father-in-law who is also a mentor.</note>, the famous genius, our teacher and rabbi Nathan, may his light shine.

And when the genius, our teacher and rabbi Nathan, saw these **new insights** <term>Hebrew: *chidushim*; original interpretations of Torah or Talmudic law that reveal new depths or applications</term>, he rejoiced in them greatly and said: "You have done well in this, that you have established and recorded these words of insight in a book. For many times a person learns and forgets, and were they not written in a book, these insights would be forgotten." 

And now they have been discussed before him, and many **dialectical arguments** <term>Hebrew: *pilpulim*; a method of intense, intricate analysis of the Talmud used to resolve contradictions</term> were said regarding them in the study hall of the **Yeshiva** <note>A traditional academy for the study of the Torah and Talmud.</note> before our master, the aforementioned famous and pious genius. And each of them gave a "reason for praise" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning they provided excellent, well-justified arguments.</note>. And now he continues to speak regarding the perfected person of high standing, the friend of my soul, of blessed memory, our teacher and rabbi Solomon; and thus my brother, the genius, our teacher and rabbi Meir, of blessed memory, added:

->**Solomon**<-
->**Until here**<- <note>original Hebrew: ע"כ (*Ad Kan*). This is a standard marker used in manuscripts to signify the end of a quote or a specific section.</note>

"Truth is in your mouth. Know that he certainly does not speak out of **faintness of heart** <note>original: *chulsha de-liba*. While it literally means a weak heart, in this context it refers to a physical state of exhaustion or illness that might exempt one from certain religious duties.</note> but rather regarding the matter of a **mortgage** <note>original: *mashkanta*. In Jewish law, this refers to a pledge of land or property given by a debtor to a creditor as security for a debt.</note> that is explained before us."

And now I have come to respond to the words of the aforementioned famous teacher and rabbi Meir, of blessed memory. It seems to me that, on the surface, his words are very puzzling. For he seemingly interpreted the words of the explicit **Gemara** <note>The section of the Talmud that contains rabbinical analysis and commentary on the Mishnah.</note>: "These are the things that have no fixed measure..." 

And from the order I saw in my books, these things have no variation at all; if so, in what did you engage in dialectics? But I go in my simplicity and say that there is no difficulty here at all. For behold, in the **Shulchan Aruch** <note>The "Set Table," the most authoritative code of Jewish law, compiled by Joseph Karo in the 16th century.</note>, in the section *Orach Chaim*, chapter 1, paragraph 1, it is written: "At first, one should strengthen himself like a lion to rise in the morning for the service of his Creator."

And the **Magen Avraham** <note>A major commentary on the Shulchan Aruch written by Rabbi Avraham Gombin (17th century), focusing on daily life and prayer.</note> wrote <gloss>there</gloss>, and these are his words: "Nevertheless, if he sees that due to weakness, etc." If so, the same law applies here in our case, for he was of a weak constitution; therefore, he permitted it in our case. These are the words of the Magen Avraham.

And regarding what the **Rama** <note>Rabbi Moses Isserles, whose glosses on the Shulchan Aruch represent the customs of Ashkenazi (Eastern European) Jews.</note> of blessed memory wrote in chapter 242 of the section *Choshen Mishpat* <note>The part of the Code of Jewish Law dealing with civil matters and finances.</note>, see there. And regarding what I wrote in my new insights on the Talmud in many places. And they already gave a "reason for praise" regarding that which we say at the beginning of the first chapter of the tractate **Chullin** <note>The Talmudic volume dealing with the laws of animal slaughter and dietary restrictions.</note>: "One who slaughters the blood, etc." and even <gloss>at the entry</gloss> and the exit. And the same law applies in every place that the **Rambam** <note>Maimonides, the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher and jurist.</note> of blessed memory wrote and ruled in his great work <note>Referring to the *Mishneh Torah*, Maimonides' comprehensive 14-volume code of Jewish Law.</note>.

His words are deep and many, but I have written in brief. From here on, I will write my words with extreme brevity, explaining the intention of the words of our Sages of blessed memory. My soul is in my request, and my Torah is in my mouth, and may the Lord, blessed be He, in His great mercy, lead me in the path of truth.

<meta>This page transitions from the personal narrative and familial debates of the previous page into a profound theological and mystical meditation on the nature of repentance. While the previous page focused on the author’s reception by his father-in-law, Rabbi Nathan, this section engages with the works of great Jewish philosophers and mystics to explore how sin is transformed into merit.</meta>

For one whose merits outweigh their iniquities, behold, this is the fruit of the path: [it is] pure. For their iniquities shall vanish like smoke, and their Torah study and their divine service shall ascend. All their toil is but a brief moment of tranquility.
Thursday, the 22nd of <unclear>Elul</unclear> <note>original: "אלול"; the final month of the Jewish calendar, dedicated to soul-searching and repentance before the New Year.</note>
---
->A Short Discourse<-

For length of days, for He is "slow to anger" <note>original: "ארוך אף"; a biblical description of God’s patience from Exodus 34:6.</note> and long-suffering, providing enough for all. I have found sages who wrote concerning the **repentance of the penitent** <term>Hebrew: *teshuvat ha-shav*; the process of a person "returning" to God after straying</term>, stating that it occupies the place where perfection was absent. This is a measure that does not arise from a strict legal obligation, but rather from the side of the Creator, blessed be He, in His attribute of goodness. For if unintentional sins were handled strictly according to their order, there would be no place for the penitent to return; however, the penitent is certain that his sins will be passed over through the love of the soul. For the confusion of those base character traits is an evil place, especially if the sin was very great. 

And it cannot be denied that it is highly possible for the penitent to reach the level of purity and be refined through the **Holy Spirit** <term>Hebrew: *Ruach HaKodesh*; divine inspiration or a state of spiritual cleanliness</term>, until there is no sin remaining in him. Justice says to me that this matter is true. However, according to the hidden heights of this matter—and especially if one walks in the ways of the Lord and His kindness—through repentance one shall rise from level to level, even reaching the rank of the **righteous person** <term>Hebrew: *Tzaddik*; a person who has never sinned or who has perfectly mastered their impulses</term> who has never sinned. 

And our master, **Rabbi Hasdai** <note>Rabbi Hasdai Crescas (c. 1340–1410), a Spanish-Jewish philosopher known for his work "The Light of the Lord" (*Or Hashem*).</note>, peace be upon him, wrote on this matter: that the righteous man contemplates his path, which is straight and paved. Through this act, a person gains an advantage, for **repentance** <note>Hebrew: *teshuvah*</note> is what leads to the **love of God**, may His name be blessed. One should be like a "mad dog" <note>A striking metaphor for relentless, obsessive pursuit. Here, it describes the penitent’s overwhelming drive to chase after the Divine to make up for lost time.</note> pursuing and prevailing, even when the "malicious waters" <note>A metaphor for overwhelming trials or sins, from Psalm 124:5.</note> try to educate him, and not merely through divine judgment. 

The "righteousness of the righteous" is a known moral concept, and he has opened his eyes toward the Creator, may His name be blessed. The knowledge of Rabbi Hasdai, of blessed memory, is great, and through this we understand that the **perfectly righteous** <term>Hebrew: *Tzaddik Gamur*</term> would not have such a gateway [of passionate return]. For we find in the Scriptures **Divine Providence** <term>Hebrew: *Hashgacha Pratit*; God's individual supervision of every creature</term> in everything that the righteous person does in his path and his piety, so that he will not sin before Him, God. All his deeds are holy, his foundation is established in strong and perfect truth in the ways of the Lord. It is proper that the power of the intellect and knowledge should prevail over the power of the senses; he should set aside physicality and merit the level of the righteous, becoming a true and worthy man of piety. 

And His pious ones—there is no difference between one and the other except for the action which "vanishes like smoke" <gloss>and he has no gateway</gloss> <note>The gloss suggests that for the righteous, the "gateway" of repentance isn't used because the sin is erased immediately through their charitable nature.</note>. All his sins are elevated through righteousness/charity. The righteous person, in his perfection on the Day of Judgment, shall merit a good life and peace, Amen, may it be His will.

All of this was written by the **ARI of blessed memory** <note>Rabbi Isaac Luria (1534–1572), the preeminent kabbalist of Safed whose teachings revolutionized Jewish mysticism.</note> in the *Book of Sermons* <note>original: "ספר דרושים"; *Sefer ha-Derushim*.</note>.

### 79
The eleventh matter: behold, we find three types of **forbearance** <note>original: "ויתור" (*vittur*); the divine act of "giving up" a claim to strict justice to allow for mercy.</note> and two types of governance within the **Attribute of Mercy** <term>Hebrew: *Middat HaRachamim*; the aspect of God that acts with compassion rather than strict judgment</term>. Scripture says: "Blessed is the Lord who chose this attribute above all levels." These three types of forbearance constitute "Kindness," the fifth governance. Corresponding to the second type of governance, he brings hints from scriptural verses, showing that the primary will of the Attribute of Mercy is to abolish and cut off evil through the "partnership of mercy." It is known that this is the ultimate goal—the nullification of all the **husks** <term>Hebrew: *Klipot*; literally "shells" or "peels" that represent the forces of spiritual impurity or evil in Kabbalistic thought</term>. Therefore, this is referred to here as the fifth level.

### 80
And "they saw the glory of the Lord" appearing within human beings through these four types of healing, as mentioned in the **Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism.</note>, Part III, 121a, in the allusion on page 102 regarding the phrase "I will be what I will be" and the four types of **Kindness** <note>original: "חסד" (*Chesed*); the Sefirah representing expansive love and benevolence.</note>. See also the **Pardes** <note>Referring to *Pardes Rimonim* (Orchard of Pomegranates) by Rabbi Moshe Cordovero, a primary systematic manual of Kabbalah.</note> in the first gate. This is what the author wrote regarding the hint of the **four Patriarchs** <note>Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and (often in this context) King David, who correspond to the Sefirot of Chesed, Gevurah, Tiferet, and Malchut.</note> mentioned above, who are:
**Kindness, Strength, Beauty, and Sovereignty** <note>original: "חסד וגבורה ותפארת ומלכות" (*Chesed, Gevurah, Tiferet, and Malchut*); the lower Sefirot that govern the manifest world.</note>.
Know that all four of these levels are contained within **Sovereignty** (*Malchut*), which is called the <term>Tree of Life</term> in terms of its totality. However, in relation to **Beauty** (*Tiferet*), they exist only within the aspect of **Understanding** (*Binah*) that stems from Beauty. Because they originate from Understanding, they ascend back to Understanding and return to the "gate" of Understanding. Therefore, they are called by the name **"The Four Who Entered the Orchard"** <note>original: "ארבעה נכנסו לפרדס"; a famous Talmudic story (Hagigah 14b) about four sages who entered the realm of mystical ascent. Here, the author interprets the story as a movement between the Sefirot.</note>, which we said previously are all contained within Sovereignty (*Malchut*). This hint will be explained further on in the explanation of the title regarding the **Unification of Sovereignty and Beauty** <note>The mystical union of the masculine (*Tiferet*) and feminine (*Malchut*) aspects of the Divine.</note> through the three hundred sixty-five branches of the <term>Tree of Life</term> <note>365 corresponds to the days of the solar year and the number of prohibitive commandments in the Torah.</note>.

Every pointer on which the descent of a ray from heaven falls, and wherever that pointer is found, we know that the sun is looking toward that direction at that time.

</margin>
Let us know the time of the day and the hour from the face of the <note>The word *qlita* likely refers here to the "plate" or a specific component of the astronomical instrument</note> instrument.
Let us raise the outer direction and place it in the inner direction, and let us see which degree descends from the angle in that inner direction. And where it descends, let us place the indicator in the middle of the two directions, and let us place the <term>pointer</term> <note>original: *kalba*; literally "the dog," the traditional technical term in Syriac and Arabic astronomy for the alidade or the sighting pointer of an astrolabe</note> upon the day which we are <margin>observing</margin>.
Let us know the hour of the night by what is known from the face of the instrument. 
Let us place the star at the time which belongs to it in the angle as it emerges, just as it is seen in the sky. 
And let us place the "wine-marker" <note>original: *hamra*; likely a specific color-coded mark or a technical term for a cursor on the instrument</note> that is in the angle from the inside, and let us place the <term>pointer</term> upon the day we are in, and let us set it to the angle that is seen at that time, just as we have observed.

---

<margin>85</margin>
When we desire to know which star is moving toward the earth, toward which direction of the directions of the place in which we are: 
Let us set the time in which we see it in the inner angle, and let us see which degree from the angle we should set for the outer angle.
For if there is a star in the inner angle, or to the left of the inner angle—or if it is necessary for us to know if there is a star from the angle, or from the left of the angle, or from the right of the angle—let us set the outer angle at that time which we see. 
As we have said: regarding the earth, that we might see it, in which direction it is at that time after the east, it emerges from the place of the instrument. 
The sky—wherever we know by what likeness it will be—the direction in which it is: if it is in the place where we see it at that time, or when it completes its journey, we shall observe it just as we have said.

But what is written in the Torah: "These are the generations of the heavens and the earth *when they were created*" <note>Genesis 2:4; the Hebrew word is *be-hibare'am*</note>.

## Chapter 9

Now we shall return to the explanation of Moses our Teacher, peace be upon him, and we will speak of what appears to us regarding the **Secret of the Work** <note>original: *Sod Ha-Melakhah*. In this context, "The Work" often refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy or the manipulation of natural forces.</note> which we are explaining, with the help of God, may He be blessed and exalted. To explain this matter in the way of the **Sages of Inquiry** <note>A common medieval term for philosophers and scientists.</note>, we must explain what Nature is and its action upon created things, and how this Nature is distinguished from **Matter** and **Form**. <note>The author is employing Aristotelian physics, which views all things as a combination of *Hyle* (underlying matter) and *Morphe* (the identifying form).</note> And lo, the human intellect becomes weary yet ascends in understanding the nature of things and these movements. If these waters were mixed in one existence, and according to the level and the intellect there would be ten types of levels; for the Intellect is that which leads the **Spheres** <term>Galgalim</term> <note>The celestial orbs in medieval cosmology that carried the planets and stars.</note> until the existence that was first, which is the **Moon**. And she is called according to their words <note>The words of the philosophers.</note>, and she is the eighth power that moves <margin>here</margin> everything; except that the spheres of the spheres are here, and these are the four <note>Likely the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>. For this level is the level of the "Work" and the material action, and the explanation of this Work—God created it according to His will. And let us not hide from our eyes that the power of this action is deficient <note>The manuscript shows a deletion here: ~~and its deficiency will be explained and the secret of the Work and it is what we shall mention with God's help~~</note>. And if the Work were strong and perfect, it would create everything just as the Creator, may He be blessed, creates everything from nothing. And the same applies to the levels mentioned, and they contain an aspect of power and nature, and it investigates and examines with the observation of its eyes. And afterwards we see the judgment according to the face of the **Wisdom of the Work** <note>Alchemy or Natural Magic.</note>, and this second observation in the explanation of the Work lacks the power of the Moon. And it is good to know that the explanation of perfection in this Moon is according to the explanation of the Intellect that moves and brings forth.

Knowledge of the **Honored Name** <term>HaShem HaNikbad</term> <note>A respectful way of referring to the Tetragrammaton or God's essential being.</note> comes from realizing that the deficiencies found within [created things] are far removed from Him. He is exalted and uplifted above all, and it is not fitting for any creature to ascribe to Him what the imagination cannot grasp. <margin>1</margin>

I have already presented certain introductions to this, and I say that investigation alone suffices to attain the first and second levels, but the **level of prophecy** cannot be attained without both investigation and deep contemplation.

Regarding the two [levels] mentioned above, it is said: If a king commanded one of his servants regarding a matter and told him, "Perform this command; if you do, I will grant you rest, and if not, I will punish you," it is not within the king's mind to do good or evil [arbitrarily]; rather, everything is determined by these actions, whether for good or for evil. The king himself does not change. Behold, it is said that **Compassion** is a trait of His, yet in Him are changing and affected actions—insofar as **"permission is granted"** <note>A reference to the concept of Free Will; while God is unchanging, human choice dictates how His light is received.</note> to man. 

All existence within the soul stands forever. Furthermore, the soul has an awakening at every time and every moment. This power exists in its perfection within the soul, which stands eternally and guides the body at every single moment. This is the meaning of the verse: "I will pass before your face," etc. <note>Exodus 33:19</note>, and so it is every day according to the power within the soul. **180** He wrote her a gift again and wrote it in a debt document <term>shtar chov</term>. So too, the Rashba <note>Rabbi Shlomo ben Aderet, a 13th-century Spanish legal authority.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, wrote in Part 3, sign 813,
and responded that it is only to "strengthen her power" <note>original: *le-yapot kocha*; a legal concept where a second document is intended to add security to a right rather than replace the first.</note>. And so wrote the Rabbi in sign <unclear>335</unclear>. And the Rabbi was correct, for since
one has already acquired it and then [the giver] returned and gave him a gift, the first is not canceled; rather, it is considered an addition.
And what was written in the Ran <note>Rabbi Nissim of Gerona, a 14th-century commentator.</note> in the chapter "Ha-Nizakin" <note>A chapter in the Talmudic tractate Gittin dealing with legal damages and documents.</note> does not contradict these words of his, for there he explained whether he had acquired it before he wrote it on the condition that he would write the document or not. And so in the Responsa of the Rashba, sign <unclear>647</unclear>, he wrote regarding one on their deathbed <term>shechiv mera</term> and one who is healthy in the case where he made and gave to her. And so the Ran explained in the chapter "Ha-Nizakin" <unclear>regarding</unclear> a gift after a gift,
implying that there are those who hold that the first gift is canceled if the first document was not dated "from today" and after it came another document; she only acquires it from that later day.
And if he wrote in the second document, "Anything that comes into my hand by virtue of my documents and my rights shall be void," then all his rights in the first document are indeed voided, and she only acquires from the second document.
This is only because he revealed his intent that it was not pleasing to him for her to acquire through the first document, <unclear>and even</unclear> if "from now" <note>original: *me’achshav*; a legal term ensuring immediate retroactive validity.</note> was written in it, since he explicitly cancels all his rights, the document is also canceled. And see the *Tur* <note>The "Four Rows," a foundational code of Jewish law by Jacob ben Asher.</note>, *Choshen Mishpat* <term>Choshen Mishpat</term> <note>The section of the law code dealing with civil and monetary matters.</note> sign <unclear>245</unclear>, regarding one who wrote a document for one person and then wrote it again for another. And the Rabbi [Joseph Karo], may his memory be a blessing, explained there in the *Beit Yosef* <note>A massive commentary on the Tur by Joseph Karo.</note> in his words that he wrote, etc.

<margin><unclear/></margin>
**101** Maimonides <note>The 12th-century philosopher and codifier, also known as Rambam.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, wrote in Chapter 9 of the Laws of Acquisition and Gifts: "The recipient of a gift who later wrote
in another document, behold, it [the gift] is void and is nothing." It is only a language of admission, and since he wrote that he retracted it, behold, it is as if he retracted before he received the gift. And in the *Tur* in sign 245, it is written
that it is like one who says "I do not desire it." And from his words, it appears that even <unclear>if</unclear> the recipient retracted
because the giver gave him something else in its place, or because the gift was canceled, it is indeed void.
And so it appears from the words of the Ran, and such is the opinion of the Rashba in the chapter "Ha-Nizakin," <unclear>whose words were brought</unclear> in the *Beit Yosef*
on the *Tur*, *Even HaEzer* <term>Even HaEzer</term> <note>The section of the law code dealing with marriage and family law.</note> sign 105, and in the *Tur*, *Choshen Mishpat* sign 245, regarding a woman who said concerning properties her husband gave her as a gift, "What he gave me is not a gift"—meaning, "I did not want this gift."
For we say that since she revealed her intent that the first gift was not pleasing to her, they [the properties] do not belong to her, and she cannot
return and acquire them unless he returns and gives her a new gift after the first was canceled.
And such is the opinion of the Rosh <note>Rabbi Asher ben Jehiel, a prominent 13th-14th century commentator.</note>. And in the Responsa of the Rashba, may his memory be a blessing, which the Rama <note>Rabbi Moses Isserles, the primary Ashkenazi authority who added glosses to the Shulchan Aruch.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, cited in *Even HaEzer*
sign 105, it says that one must cancel the second gift, for the first gift was canceled entirely, <unclear>and so it is</unclear>.

**100a** If he returned and gave another document, he has acquired from the first and not from the last. If he returned and gave
another document against his [the recipient's] will, the first gift is not void unless he revealed his intent
that he was not pleased with this one and wanted the other, as the Rabbi [Rama], may his memory be a blessing, wrote in the glosses of the *Shulchan Aruch* in sign 245. <unclear>They learned</unclear>
this from Nachmanides <note>A leading 13th-century Spanish scholar, also known as Ramban.</note>: specifically when he returned and gave another document, and not when he returned and canceled the first gift. But this is not the place [to expand].

Now we will respond to the first resolution that we brought on the previous page, and these are its words: <gloss>And if you say</gloss>—regarding the difficulty raised by the aforementioned resolution, why did he not say "and he borrowed it" <note>Referring to the specific legal category of a borrower (*shoel*), who carries a high level of liability.</note>, for behold, there is a loss <term>psida</term> <note>A financial loss or detriment that affects legal liability.</note> that we do not have? It is not difficult at all. <gloss>The author says</gloss> that he would explain "and he borrowed it" in a context similar to a typical borrowing, which implies both a case of loss and a case of no loss. And it is not difficult at all, since he would explain "and he borrowed it" in this way; Rava <note>A leading 4th-century Babylonian sage and head of the academy at Mahuza.</note> spoke well when he said it implies both a case of loss and a case of no loss, since it was found that it was in <gloss>meaning, in his house</gloss>.

These matters were not heard by those who said it is not difficult at all. <gloss>The author says</gloss> according to what he concludes later, these [cases] were said regarding improvements, which they say alongside hidden matters. And now he see that he did not say "I was diligent and did not neglect it," <note>original: *shakidna velo pashiana*; a standard legal declaration for a bailee claiming they were not at fault.</note> for in a case of theft, he would be exempt according to everyone. He hears and sees that he explains it thus. Rather, it is not difficult at all regarding that which Rava said—that it was found in his possession—and Rav Hisda and Rav Huna also stand according to the previously mentioned Mishnah <note>The foundational legal code of the Oral Torah.</note>, and not like Rav Huna in the name of Rav. Regarding those of the House of the Exilarch <note>The political head of the Jewish community in Babylonian exile.</note>, it implies that they hold like Rav Huna, that an oath alone allows one to collect [the claim]. And those of the House of Rav Huna mentioned above [said]: "I swear that I was not negligent," and the borrower died in this [state], and he said to them, "See the seized item that was found with him." And he would say there was no loss at all for us.

And one cannot say that even in such a case he explains that there is no loss, but rather he says that he is the Master's messenger <term>shelicha d'mara</term> and is exempt; for if so, it would be difficult, as it is simple that he is exempt. Rather, here where Rava says it was found with him, he is exempt because the Master was present. If so, all of them are specifically in a case of loss, and for this reason Rava said he is exempt. For if he had not said he was exempt, then he would have to swear and pay in such a case where there was no loss.

<margin>104</margin> Alternatively, it can be resolved that he specifically chose the wording "I was diligent and did not neglect it" because this was his own loss, and the borrower ~~increases his own loss~~ <gloss>for we require a matter that is visible to the eye</gloss>.

And from this a difficulty arises: what did Rava mean by saying ~~it was found in his possession~~? Perhaps Rava is dealing with a case where it was not his own loss, but rather he was exempt because the Master was present; in such a case he spoke well [when saying] "and he borrowed it" in a context similar to a typical borrowing.

<margin>106</margin> Alternatively, it can be resolved that perhaps here where he said "I was diligent and did not neglect it," he had the legal presumption of being the Master's messenger. For this reason Rava said "I was diligent and did not neglect it" <gloss>because it was his own loss</gloss>. But if he had not admitted it was his own loss, but instead said there was a presumption of the Master being present, in such a case he would be exempt even though it was not his own loss. And examine this well.

<margin>107</margin> Alternatively, it can be resolved that he specifically chose the wording "I was diligent" because that would be his own loss. That is, if the borrower had not died and had taken an oath, he would be causing his own loss by swearing falsely. Behold, since it is a loss in my own matter, and he says he is exempt, so too in a case where there is no loss at all, he would also be exempt.

<margin>108</margin> Alternatively, it can be resolved that he specifically chose "I was diligent" because it is a much greater legal presumption, since he himself stands before us and swears. And regarding what Rava said, "it was found with him," behold, there was not such a great presumption as a borrower who stands before us and swears "I was diligent and did not neglect it." For this reason Rava said that even if "it was found with him," he is exempt as well.

<margin>109</margin> Alternatively, it can be resolved that here it is different because he has this presumption: perhaps he himself saw that it died, and because he saw it, he said "I was diligent and did not neglect it." Even though he did not see it at the moment of death, nevertheless, since he said this...

<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on personal status (the "Shetuki" and "Cutheans") and witness reliability, citing Rabbi Simchah and Rabbi Yehudah Gaon. This page shifts the focus toward civil law and judicial ethics, specifically the judge’s role in facilitating compromise and the authority of communal custom over formal Torah law regarding transaction fees.</meta>

And so it is fitting for every judge who deals with litigants: if there are two litigants who must pay the court fees, or if there is some dispute or matter between them and they come before the judge for a ruling, the judge should use his intellect to examine the division of the matter—who is liable and who is exempt. If the judge sees that there is an element of doubt, or if he sees that the negotiation is more a matter of dispute than of strict law, he should seek to cast a compromise upon them and issue a ruling on the financial matter. He should speak to their hearts gently and with sound advice until everyone agrees to what the teacher says. For in this way, he makes his path and their path successful. Regarding this, <term>our Sages of blessed memory</term> said: "One who effects a compromise is praiseworthy." This is especially true in this place where the law is not so clear or well-known; and in a place close to peace, there the blessing shall dwell. So too, it is fitting for every upright judge and teacher to make peace with all his strength, and his actions should be for the sake of Heaven—and this is sufficient for one who understands.

And regarding that which <term>Rema</term> <note>Rabbi Moses Isserles (1520–1572), the primary authority for Ashkenazi Jewish law</note> of blessed memory wrote in his gloss, as follows:
> However, the common custom among neighbors is that the seller pays the scribe's fee.

This was also written in the responsa of <term>Rashba</term> <note>Rabbi Shlomo ben Aderet (1235–1310), a preeminent Spanish legal authority</note> of blessed memory, as follows:
> And you asked about the custom practiced in Spain, and among us as well, that the buyer does not pay the scribe’s fee, but the seller alone. Answer: Know that this is a beautiful and proper custom, and in a place where they have adopted a custom, they follow it. Nonetheless, according to Torah law, the matter is not so; rather, the buyer pays the fee, etc.

So too wrote <term>Rosh</term> <note>Rabbi Asher ben Jehiel (c. 1250–1327), a German scholar who moved to Spain</note> of blessed memory and the *Tur* <note>The *Arba'ah Turim*, a 14th-century legal code</note> of blessed memory in *Choshen Mishpat* <note>The section of the law code dealing with civil and financial matters</note> section 67; see there. The reasoning for this is that the buyer is the one who benefits from the acquisition of the deed, as it remains in his hand as evidence and to secure his rights; therefore, the law dictates that he should pay the fee. However, in a place where there is a custom, we follow the custom, even if it is against an absolute Torah law, for "custom cancels law" <note>original: "Minhag mevatel Halakha"</note>. And so <term>our Sages of blessed memory</term> said: "Everything follows the local custom."

---

118. A question for the teacher: Can the judge effect a compromise between them even though the litigants did not explicitly agree, but merely remained silent? Answer: I have already written in the previous response that at the time of a compromise, everything must be by the will of both parties, for without their will, "compromise" does not apply. And as for what <term>Rambam</term> <note>Maimonides (1138–1204), the great philosopher and codifier</note> of blessed memory wrote:
> And even though he has already brought the law to its truth, it is a commandment to mediate.

This refers specifically to when he has an open door to speak to their hearts so they will agree to the compromise with a willing soul. But through coercion? Heaven forbid a judge should do such a thing! For "let the law pierce the mountain" <note>original: "HaDin yakov et ha-har"; a legal maxim meaning that strict law should be applied regardless of the consequences</note>. A judge must be very careful in this, lest—God forbid—he perverts the judgment of a companion. His eye and heart should always be toward truth and uprightness; the eye of God is upon him, and all his deeds shall succeed.

# Regarding the First Side, Numbered, and the End of the Script
### The Year "And the Lord showed him a tree" <note>original: "Vayorehu Etz" (Exodus 15:25). This phrase is being used both as a biblical reference and likely as a chronogram for a specific year.</note>

It is said as an interpretation of this that the names of the angels, such as **Samuel** and others like **Azriel**, are known. They asked: since it seems they were not sent in this matter <note>referring to a specific mystical or biblical event</note>, "And the Lord showed him a tree," and He placed it... according to the interpretation of the verse, one may write it with the letters of **Chabad** <term>Chabad</term> <note>An acronym for the intellectual attributes: Chochmah (Wisdom), Binah (Understanding), and Da’at (Knowledge)</note> within the thirty-two <note>likely referring to the thirty-two paths of wisdom</note>, perhaps my brother, etc.

In the seventh portion, and in another interpretation, it is recorded that it is therefore permitted to write it within the **Torah** <term>Torah</term> <note>The Five Books of Moses, specifically referring here to the laws of writing a kosher scroll</note>. However, if one gives them to the Lion <note>original: "Aryeh," possibly referring to the Ari, Rabbi Isaac Luria, the foundational figure of modern Kabbalah</note>, he said in *The Delights of Wisdom* that it is forbidden because it contains the secret of the window in peace. One must erase the word if it was written on the side of the Torah scroll, even though it was written in the book of their names. At the end, there are intertwined words in the book, and one must erase them on the **Sabbath** <note>This is a complex legal point, as erasing is generally forbidden on the Sabbath; it likely refers to a specific circumstance or a mystical "erasing"</note>.

Blessed be the one who goes forth. Also, in the commentary of the **RAMAZ** <term>RAMAZ</term> <note>Rabbi Moses Zacuto (1625–1697), a prominent Kabbalist and poet known for his work on scribal laws and mystical names</note> on the portion of **Shemaya**, "and He showed him." According to the commentator, this is the explanation of the deed, and from here perhaps I stood, and at its end "He will show him." It appears that there is nothing else in the two interpretations in the Prophets or in the books of the "Order of the World" <note>original: "Seder Olam"</note>. "And He showed him" appears in the Torah, and he also said this is a hint to **Moses** <unclear>his house</unclear> in Heaven. It can be written with the "Heaven below" in the measure of **Samuel**.

# Introduction

And this is the law of man: to bring up gleanings and to collect the interpretations of the early masters of 
**Kabbalah** <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>The received tradition of Jewish mysticism.</note> who spoke of the mysteries of God through their hints, and who built a foundation and handbreadth
for the principles in the secret of the justifications according to the Word. In it is a lovely and pleasant explanation. And behold, these 
were called "Souls" along with holy Names called the Holy **Shekhinah** <term>Shekhinah</term> <note>The feminine aspect of the Divine Presence, often considered the immanent part of God that dwells in the world.</note>, which is called *Kinneret* <note>original: "Kinneret," the Hebrew name for the Sea of Galilee, here used as a mystical designation.</note>.
<margin>And this is the secret of the three lines</margin>
And between them is the aspect of the three holy lines, namely: 1. **Father** <term>Ab</term> 2. **Son** <term>Ben</term> 3. **Holy Spirit** <term>Ruach HaKodesh</term> <note>This identifies the "Three Lines" of the Sefirotic tree with the Christian Trinity, a hallmark of Christian Kabbalah.</note>. And this is the secret
of saying "Blessed be the Lord who sent His holy spirit and will to illuminate the hearts of my brothers and friends."
And since these three aforementioned holy lines are life, the first is for the sake of the Shekhinah called *Kinneret*. 
And behold, the aspect of the three aforementioned holy lines are in the aspect of the **Infinite** <term>Ein Sof</term> <note>God as He is in Himself, prior to any emanation or creation.</note>, blessed be He, before the world was created.
And these holy lines were included in one another, a secret of Wisdom hidden from all living things. Except
that the Blessed Name emanated His light into the place of the **Contraction** <term>Tzimtzum</term> <note>The Kabbalistic concept of God "shrinking" or withdrawing His infinite light to create a "vacant space" where the universe could exist.</note>, and then the **Impression** <term>Reshimu</term> <note>The "residue" or "trace" of divine light that remained in the vacuum after the Tzimtzum.</note> was revealed in the secret of the three aforementioned holy
<margin>within the void</margin>
lines. And this is what I said: that within the aforementioned vacant space, there a single thin beam illuminated
in the aspect of the aforementioned "Line," in which all the existence of the worlds was revealed in the secret of all the **Sefirot** <term>Sefirot</term> <note>The ten divine attributes or emanations through which the Infinite interacts with the universe.</note>
mentioned above in the aforementioned secret. And the secret of all the "Levels" is rooted in holiness. And this is 
what is written in the *Book of Creation*: "Ten Sefirot of nothingness: ten and not nine, ten and not eleven."
And behold, in these three aforementioned holy lines, the light of the Infinite, blessed be He, is clothed in the aspect of
**Crown** (Keter), **Wisdom** (Chochmah), and **Understanding** (Binah), which are the three ancient lines, and they are the emanators for all the emanated beings
which the Blessed Name created in the aforementioned secret. And the secret of all the levels is rooted in holiness. And this is 
the spirit of the Shekhinah that rests upon them in the secret of "the Shekhinah dwells between them," the aspect 
of the "Man Above" <note>original: "Adam shel Ma'ala," referring to the primordial divine man, Adam Kadmon.</note> who influences the illumination by way of those five aforementioned aspects called Shekhinah.
<margin>Souls</margin>
And there are with Him seven [and] five: the spirit of the Shekhinah that rests upon them in the secret of "the Shekhinah dwells between them,"
the aspect of the three aforementioned holy lines and the five aforementioned holy names. There they are the aspect of the five 
vessels, which are the three *Sefirot of Nothingness* of which we spoke. And the secret of these introductions will be explained 
each detail in its place, with the help of God. And the Lord shall add two [and] one: a thousand, eight thousand, and eighteen thousand worlds
mentioned in the secret of "He who rides in the heavens [Arvot], the Lord is His name," etc. And now we shall return to the explanation of the aforementioned holy chapter
concerning the matter of the three aforementioned holy lines called **Father, Son, and Holy Spirit**, etc.

Some time has passed—about three weeks—since I finished dealing with the matter of the aforementioned letter. Since then, we have heard nothing further, nor has any reply reached us from the Rabbi, may he live and be well. But today, his pure and just letter reached us. And after the death of the Great Rabbi, our honored teacher and master, Rabbi Isaac Arditti—may his memory be for the life of the world to come—on Monday, the fifteenth of the month of Shevat <term>The eleventh month of the Hebrew calendar, usually falling in January or February</term>, in the year 5547 <note>1787 CE</note>, the entire congregation wept for him as a festive multitude, and they came to the place of Phinehas <note>A poetic or local reference to the burial grounds</note> and buried him with great honor and eulogized him with a great lamentation. And most of the congregation came to the house of the aforementioned Rabbi, and all of them with one voice chose his son—the distinguished and honored teacher and master, Rabbi Solomon Arditti, may his Light shine—to fill the place of his father, may his memory be for the life of the world to come. And the entire congregation agreed to give him fifty gersh <term>A unit of Ottoman currency, also known as a piaster</term> every week.

On Sunday, the 28th of Adar <term>The twelfth month of the Hebrew calendar</term>, in the year 5551 <note>1791 CE</note>, the soul of our master, the Great Rabbi, our honored teacher and master, Rabbi Joseph Nahum, came to its rest—may his memory be for the life of the world to come. And the entire congregation, may their Maker protect them, eulogized him with a great lamentation. And at the time of the funeral bier, the entire congregation came and appointed and accepted upon themselves the eminent Rabbi, his son—the distinguished and honored teacher and master, Rabbi Isaac Nahum, may his Light shine—to fill the place of his father, may his memory be for the life of the world to come, for all matters of the congregation just as his father had been. And they received him with a friendly countenance on that day with the goodwill of the congregation, and they gave him fifty gersh for every week.

On Wednesday, the 19th of Kislev <term>The ninth month of the Hebrew calendar, usually falling in November or December</term>, in the year 5552 <note>Late 1791 CE</note>, the soul of our master, the Great Rabbi, our honored teacher and master, Rabbi Solomon Arditti, came to its rest—may his memory be for the life of the world to come—and the congregation accepted his son, our honored teacher and master, Rabbi Isaac Arditti, may his Light shine.

---

This law is simple: anyone who uses the house of another with his permission, and afterward, while the person is still inside, the owner wishes to evict him—we do not listen to the owner until the fixed time between them has ended. And even if he gave it to him indefinitely without fixing a time, he cannot evict him at any hour he wishes; rather, he must provide him thirty days' notice beforehand so that he may find another house. This is explained in the Talmudic chapter "He Who Hires Laborers" <note>Referencing Bava Metzia 101a</note>. This law applies in all places, and so it is written in the Shulchan Aruch <term>The "Set Table," the foundational code of Jewish Law</term>, Section 312, and so all the legal decisors have written. It is a simple matter and a general rule in the laws of congregations: that anyone whom the congregation has accepted to have authority <term>Hebrew: "Serarah," referring here to a rabbinic or administrative post</term> over them, they cannot retract their decision except in the case of a major grievance. And even if the congregation itself accepted him by a majority vote, they cannot retract; how much more so if the entire congregation accepted him with one voice, as appears to be the case in the matter before us.

---

Let us return to our subject: since the congregation agreed with one voice to appoint the Rabbi, our honored teacher and master, Rabbi Isaac Nahum, may his Light shine, and they filled the place of his father, may his memory be for the life of the world to come, and they accepted him upon themselves and appointed him a salary of fifty gersh for every week, the congregation cannot retract their decision and are obligated to give him the aforementioned salary all the days of his life. May his merit protect us, Amen.

---

Another law pertains to this: on the 8th of the month of Shevat in the year 5553 <note>1793 CE</note>, there came before us the honored and distinguished Isaac HaLevi, may his Light shine, and he spoke in this language: "I admit before you that my house, which is free of all claims and <unclear>debt-burdens</unclear>, belongs to the <unclear>widow</unclear> of the <unclear>holy society</unclear>. On the aforementioned <unclear>day</unclear>, I gave her permission to dwell in my aforementioned house all the days of <unclear>her life</unclear>, and in exchange for this, <unclear>she</unclear> <unclear>obligates herself</unclear> <unclear>every</unclear> <unclear>week</unclear> <unclear>to study</unclear> <unclear>Mishnah</unclear> <note>The earliest layer of the Talmud; here likely referring to the tradition of studying for the merit of the soul</note> etc."

<meta>This page continues the shift from the previous page's historical Jewish records into a dense Syriac theological treatise. While the prior page introduced the concept of the "Word" (Logos) and the soul's existence, this page focuses intensely on the "Dispensation" or divine plan and the specific nature of the Word's birth and existence—arguing that the Word shares the nature of God rather than a physical, corporeal nature.</meta>

In these words concerning the Dispensation <note>original: 'mdabranutha' — a technical term for God’s "economy" or plan for the world</note> regarding that which was "In the Beginning" <note>original: 'ba-reshit' — a reference to the first words of Genesis</note>, not in the way that it happened to be governed by it,
but in the way it happened to be created by one single, immediate Word. For just as He said, "Let there be light," and there was light,
so also from the category of the Word it happened according to the way God willed to create all things—not in the way 
known to us from the source <note>original: 'taditha' — literally 'breast,' here used metaphorically for a nurturing source of knowledge</note>, how or by what thought God created everything that He created. 
Because that which God brings forth from the Creator has no "sisterhood" <note>original: 'akhetha' — implying a peer-level relationship or similarity</note> between them; rather, it is by the Word of God, not 
as a created thing, but truly from God, the Begetter of the Word. After all these things we have said concerning the Confession <term>Tawditha: a formal statement of faith or creed</term> of the Truth:
If a person should ask, "How does the Word exist?" or "How was He born? In what manner of nature <note>original: 'gnes' — from the Greek 'genos,' meaning kind, race, or category</note> 
or by what corporeal measure?" let us say to him that if the Word came as a birth from the nature of God, He has no manner 
of body. Because God the Word exists in the nature of God, and there is no nature of the body in Him, but rather the nature of God 
is the Word. And the nature of God, as it exists, is not from the nature of the body but exists within the nature of God.
Because the Word is God, not from the nature of the body, but He exists within the nature of God. And for the Word, God, 
whenever He goes forth or is born from the nature of God, He has no corporeal measure because He is God. 
Just as the nature of the body is born through a corporeal birth, so also from God, the nature of God is born by the Word—
which is the Word of God. And the nature of God has no corporeal measure, but exists within the nature
of God. And because He is of the nature of God, there is no corporeal measure in Him, because God 
is the Word. And if a person should ask <insert>#</insert> in what nature, or how, or what is 
the Word of God, let that [question] be silenced, just as we have said. Because the Word of God has no manner 
of body, because God is the Word, and there is no nature of the body in Him, but rather the nature of God exists [in Him].
Because God is the Word, and the nature of God, as it exists, is not of the nature of the body, but exists within the nature 
of God. And for the Word, God, whenever He goes forth or is born from the nature of God, He has no measure

in what nature, or how, or what is the Word of God, let it be silenced as we have said,

because the Word of God has no manner of body, because God is the Word, and there is no
