Very good. And behold, I saw a man of Israel who had several sons, and he gave them instructions in his final testament. He told them, "my sons," and requested that they read from a specific order of books, and that they should read from them regularly. In one of those books, the name of a wicked person was mentioned. Now, he had one son who was wise and distinguished. When this son read in that book and reached the name <margin>the name of the wicked</margin> of the wicked man, he did not want to read it aloud. He said to his father, "What shall I do? If I read the name of a wicked man in this place, what punishment will fall upon me?" He did not wish to mention the name of the wicked in that place, so he questioned his father. His father replied, "This judgment is sufficient," <note>This implies the father approved of the son's pious hesitation to utter an evil name.</note> and the father acted in this manner afterward with all the other sons.

### 157
<margin>157</margin> You know what the <term>Philosopher</term> <note>original: 'ha-Philosoph' — typically referring to Aristotle in medieval Jewish texts</note> said in his explanation of the *Book of Animals* <note>Likely Aristotle’s 'History of Animals' or 'On the Parts of Animals', which were widely studied in the Middle Ages</note>. He brought a story of a certain man who said to a Sage: "Sage, why does this world appear to me as nothing more than a fine speck of dust floating before the wind?" The Sage answered him and said: "Because you have seen the light of the Upper World. Therefore, this world appears to you as darkness. But in the eyes of other people, the whole world appears as light because they themselves are in darkness." For one who is inside the darkness sees nothing but darkness and imagines it is light. But one who stands in the light and looks at the darkness knows truly that it is darkness. And so, behold, I saw a man who had sons, and he was rebuking them regarding their affairs, explaining how any loss comes to them because they dwell within the body—which is within the darkness—yet they imagine they are dwelling within a great light. They do not know what is light and what is darkness until they depart from the body and see the clear and pure light. Know that all souls are beneath the <term>Upper World</term> <note>original: 'Olam ha-Elyon' — the spiritual or divine realm</note>. In this world, the soul sees nothing but a semblance of the true form. It is like the moon, which receives light from the sun but is itself dark. The light found in the moon is nothing but a reflection of the sun's light. That light shines upon the <term>Lower World</term> <note>original: 'Olam ha-Shafel' — the physical, material earth</note>, and by it we walk. But we do not see the light of the sun itself, because our eyes are too weak to behold that great light. Furthermore, you know that the soul is from the Upper World and the body is from the Lower World. The "Form" is the soul, and the body is the <term>Matter</term> <note>original: 'ha-Chomer' — the physical substance of the world</note>. The Form governs the Matter, and if there is no Form, Matter cannot stand at all. Everything that the soul sees in this world is only in the proportion of a shadow. There is a great light from the Upper Light that shines upon it, and through it the soul sees what it sees. If the soul were to depart from the body, it would see by that light itself and would be very joyful and cling to it. As long as it is within the body, it sees only <unclear>according to</unclear> the power of the body, which cannot endure more than this. In this <unclear>trusts</unclear> man, <unclear>and regarding</unclear> this it is said...

<summary>This page uses a parable about a pious son and a philosophical dialogue to explain that the physical world is merely a shadow or "darkness" compared to the spiritual "Upper World." The year 5493 <note>1732–1733 CE</note>, here in the city of **Meknes**, may God protect her. On the Holy Sabbath, the 18th of the month of Marheshvan <note>late October or early November</note>, while the entire congregation was in the synagogue before the reading of the Torah scroll, there was a great earthquake.

Several houses collapsed, and the roads were filled with <unclear>stones</unclear>. From that great earthquake, men, women, and children died—may the Merciful One protect us. Despite all this, "His anger is not turned away, and His hand is still stretched out" <note>A quote from Isaiah 9:11, often used in Jewish liturgy and chronicles to suggest that divine punishment has not yet ceased</note>, heaven forbid. Every day and night there were small tremors for about ten consecutive days.

On Tuesday, the 11th of the month of Kislev, the earthquake returned a second time in the middle of the night. On Thursday, the 8th of the month of Shevat <note>roughly January</note>, it returned a third time in the middle of the night—a very great earthquake. Several shops fell, as did a lodging place of the Gentiles <note>original: 'goyim' — referring here to the local non-Jewish, likely Muslim, population</note>, and many houses within the city and in the **Mellah** <term>Mellah</term> <note>The walled Jewish quarter of a Moroccan city</note>. Many lives among the Gentiles were lost under the rubble. Every single time the earthquake came, the entire congregation would go out to the fields and sleep there in the open and among the orchards. May God have mercy on them and on all Israel, and may He be filled with compassion for us, Amen, may it be His will.

And another:
On Tuesday, the 26th of the month of Tevet in the year 5496 <note>January 1736 CE</note>, the earthquake returned again—a very great earthquake in the middle of the night. The congregation went out to the fields and slept there in the field in the frost and the great cold. May God have mercy on us, Amen, may it be His will. Every single day there were small tremors, by day and by night.

All the people were sleeping and they woke up in a great panic. Several walls fell, along with some houses inside the city, in the **Mellah**, and in the field. The entire congregation was running and fleeing to the fields, and some people went toward the city gate and stayed there all night in the great cold and the snow.

On Tuesday, the 3rd of the month of Shevat, after midnight, there was a great earthquake. After the earthquake, there was a very great storm wind in the world. Likewise, the earthquake occurred several more times, sometimes by day and sometimes by night. May God have mercy.

<meta>This page represents a significant shift in both language and content from the previous folio. While the prior page contained a Hebrew Kabbalistic discussion on the hierarchy of angels and the creation of the firmament, this page is written in Syriac. # The Commentary on the Tradition of the Gospel of Saint Matthew the Apostle

It is said that after a period of thirty years had been completed, while he was in Syria, he went out to preach among the nations <note>original: 'ʿammē', referring to the Gentiles or non-Jewish peoples</note>. It seemed right to others that he should write for them in the language of the Hebrews <note>original: 'ʿebrāyē'</note> because he was among them. And from the "gold" <note>A metaphor for pure or precious teachings</note> of what he said to the Lord, it was asked that he give to them the inheritance of eternal life. Just as Hannah <note>likely referring to the Prophetess Hannah, or possibly a reference to the 'gold' of the genealogy</note> spoke by her hand, those who came after Christ saw that which was revealed. For this was what happened afterward; and thus it was translated for the nations so that the reverence of Christ might be revealed and made known to them. 

And in this latter part, because this explanation is for those to whom it was written—namely, the Jews—a brother desired that they should know that he is indeed the Christ <term>Christ</term> <note>From the Greek 'Christos', meaning 'Anointed One'; in Syriac, 'Mshikha'</note> who was promised to their fathers, beginning from Abraham and David. Because of this, he began his genealogy <note>original: 'tawdītā'</note> from those who are like his roots. He began from Abraham and continued until Joseph. He maintained this because this explanation covers the time from when they were in Egypt and the kingdom of the Jews was established. Matthew the Apostle, in this Gospel which he wrote, traced the line from Abraham until Joseph, the husband of Mary, from whom was born Jesus who is called Christ.

He says then, after these things, that these are the generations from Abraham until Christ: fourteen generations; and from the Babylonian captivity until Christ: fourteen generations. And in this explanation, just as there were human kings, there were also faithful men; through these, Christ was born in the time of those who came later. Or rather, others were born from the "gold" of Hannah's line, and another from Joseph, who was born of David, so that Christ might come from the seed of David.

->The Explanation of the Way<-

First is the word of the Gospel of Matthew. In it is seen the will of God, which was the Word that was from the beginning with God <note>A reference to the opening of the Gospel of John, here harmonized with Matthew's intent</note>. This is the genealogy of Christ: from Abraham until David, and from David to the Babylonian captivity, and from the captivity to Christ. This is so that one may see another aspect of the light of righteousness revealed in Christ toward everyone. 

For this reason, he began with the lineage of Abraham, the father of the nations and a vessel of righteousness, and he included David, the father of kings and the Anointed of God. In this word, which is spoken simply, he gives to us the birth of Christ as it was written; for that word is from the Holy Spirit, and it is from the Word that existed from of old. He maintained this so that it would be visible to men that he is the Christ at the time he became a child of flesh and blood. And he held to this because it says "Abraham begat Isaac, and Isaac begat Jacob, and Jacob begat Judah and his brothers." 

After these, he names other men who were among them, those who were of the royal lineage. And after these was born Joseph, the husband of Mary, from whom was born Jesus. He appeared to men as being from the seed of David; yet he was God who became flesh and dwelt among us, as John the Apostle said. And from this foundation, Matthew spoke in this Gospel.

**Vocabulary:** Explanation, Gospel, Matthew, Abraham, David, Genealogy, Joseph, Christ, Jesus

<summary>This page provides a Syriac commentary on the Gospel of Matthew, explaining the theological significance of Jesus's genealogy and his descent from Abraham and David to prove his messiahship to the Jewish people.</summary>
<keywords>Gospel of Matthew, genealogy of Jesus, Abraham, King David, Syriac Christianity, New Testament commentary, Incarnation</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the Syriac Christian commentary of the previous folio back to a Hebrew philosophical and theological discourse. # Solomon spoke: Behold, it has already been explained in the first part a little concerning the matter of the soul and its parts. I have expanded somewhat in this 
place because it is the great principle and the honorable pillar upon which all the laws of the Torah and the commandments are built.
The knowledge of the High One, blessed be He, and the knowledge of the soul leads to the knowledge of the Creator, blessed be He, as it is said: "From my flesh I shall see God" <note>original: 'mibsari echezeh eloh'; a quote from Job 19:26, used here to suggest that by understanding one's own physical and spiritual nature, one can gain insight into the Divine</note>. And likewise, the soul possesses free choice; for if we were not masters of choice, we would not be deserving of punishment 
or reward. Also, because the soul is spiritual and is the most honorable part of all the parts of man, I shall therefore explain it a little 
according to the necessity of this place. And the "Perfect Guide" <note>A common title for Maimonides (Rambam), referring to his work 'The Guide for the Perplexed'</note>, peace be upon him, said in the first chapter of what he prefaced to the Book of 
*Abot* <note>Referring to Maimonides' 'Eight Chapters,' his introduction to the Ethics of the Fathers (Pirkei Avot)</note> that the soul is one, but it has only five powers—and he called them "parts" so that they might be understood. He therefore reckoned 
the five aforementioned powers as: the **Nutritive** part, the **Sensitive** part, the **Imaginative** part, the **Appetitive** part, 
and the **Rational** part. And behold, Maimonides, of blessed memory, categorized these powers generally as they pertain to the human soul, 
and afterwards, he began to detail them. He explained that the **Nutritive** part is the power by which the body grows and replaces what is diminished from it, 
and it has seven powers. The **Sensitive** part is well-known: it is the power of sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch. 
These comprise the totality of all the parts, according to what the "Perfect Guide," of blessed memory, informed us.

---

-> SECTION KRA"N <- <note>This likely refers to a specific source or a division in the manuscript's organization</note>

According to what appears in the "Science of Demonstration" <note>Likely referring to Aristotelian logic or formal philosophy</note>, the human power that produces, grows, and nourishes is called "soul," according to 
the levels of the three known souls. And behold, all these powers are powers of the human body, and in them 
man shares a nature with the animal and the plant; these are called the **animal soul** <term>Animal soul</term> <note>Hebrew: 'Nefesh HaBehamit'; the part of the psyche responsible for biological functions and physical desires</note>. All these powers are material 
and are transformed or destroyed with the destruction of the "subject" <note>The physical body</note> in which these powers dwell. Concerning these, it is said that the spirit 
of man goes upward and the spirit of the animal goes downward <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 3:21</note>.

---

-> SECTION DK"D <-

Behold, it has been explained that these powers are the earthly, material parts. However, the "breath of the spirit of life" 
given to man is a "portion of God from above" <note>original: 'chelek eloha mima’al'; a phrase from Job 31:2</note>. It is eternal and spiritual; it is not like the soul of living creatures, nor is it attached 
to the composite physical power at all, but rather it dwells within it to lead it and guide it in the straight path. This is what is called **Neshama** <term>Neshama</term> <note>The highest level of the soul in Jewish thought, representing the divine intellect and eternal spark</note>. 
Indeed, some men of wisdom call it the *Neshama*, and they call the **Rational** part which we mentioned before a part 
of the animal soul, because both are intellectual and are not material. The difference between them is that the Intellect 
is a power attached to the intellectual *Neshama*; it is that which perceives, understands, and guides the power of the body, just as the soul 
relates to the body. It is a spiritual and eternal creation, not attached to the body at all. That same *Neshama* is itself the 
leading and honorable power that conducts itself within the body in a "covenant of peace," and it stands for ever and ever, never ceasing 
at all. Regarding this *Neshama*, the scripture says, "And the soul of man became a living soul," etc. <note>Genesis 2:7</note> <unclear>etc.</unclear> And so it is said 
that the spirit of man is the *Neshama*, and it is not proper to call it by the name *Nefesh* <note>The lower soul</note>, but only by the names *Ruach* and *Neshama*.

-> SECTION KRA"N <-

All the things which we have mentioned in this matter are straightforward to the one who understands and right to those who find knowledge. And because 
the words of the Sages, of blessed memory, have come down to us regarding this matter, they called the soul of man by five names, as found in the *Midrash Rabbah* <note>A collection of ancient homiletic interpretations of the Torah; the five names are usually listed as Nefesh, Ruach, Neshama, Chaya, and Yechida</note>.

<vocab>Nefesh (Lower Soul), Neshama (Divine Breath), Maimonides (Rambam), The Perfect Guide, Five Powers of the Soul, Free Will, Spirituality, Materiality, Eight Chapters, Midrash Rabbah</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the Jewish philosophical understanding of the soul, heavily influenced by Maimonides. It distinguishes between the material "animal soul" shared with other living beings and the eternal, divine "Neshama" that provides humans with intellect and moral guidance.</summary>

->Voice<-

<margin>Peace be upon him</margin>
A lamentation is heard for Rabbi Moshe Ibn Yahya <note>The deceased individual; "Ibn Yahya" is a prominent Sephardic Jewish family name</note>, may his memory be for a blessing in the life of the world to come.
<margin>Moshe Ibn Yahya</margin>
A voice of terror, of sudden dread! Behold, the soul that was given to him in purity has been returned to God. At midnight, death came upon him suddenly, for the radiance of his face was altered; his thoughts became confused, and he was deeply pained and grieving for his soul, aching over the weight of his sins and transgressions. But consider the righteous judgment of the Just One <note>Refers to God as the ultimate, fair judge of human life</note>. Those who loved him did this for his merit, according to their ability at that hour, and he did all that the LORD our God commanded him. May his memory be for good and for a blessing. May he find grace and kindness according to the kindness and charity he performed with other people. For the kindness of the LORD is from everlasting to everlasting upon those who fear Him, and His righteousness unto children’s children <note>A quote from Psalm 103:17, emphasizing the generational rewards of a pious life</note>. Such was his lot, according to my humble opinion; may his memory be for good and for a blessing. This is how it appeared to my own eyes. He departed from the world with a good name in the year five thousand four hundred and sixteen from the Creation <note>1656 CE</note>, having lived eighty-eight years.
<margin>The Book of the Pious</margin>
At every time and moment, he thought of his soul and would confess with the <term>Vidui: a formal prayer of confession and repentance, often recited on one's deathbed</term>, as it is written in the *Book of the Pious* <note>original: 'Sefer Hasidim'; a central 12th-century Hebrew text on ethics, piety, and customs by Rabbi Judah he-Hasid</note>. He comforted himself over his sins and returned in complete repentance before his Creator. And there was evening and there was morning, one day <note>A reference to the Creation narrative in Genesis 1:5, here likely symbolizing the completion of a full life cycle</note>. Let us all together bless him and recount his praise according to my poor understanding. May it be the will of the God of Heaven that his merit stands for us and for all of Israel, Amen.

->Voice<-

They shall enter into peace; they shall rest in their beds, each one who walks in his uprightness <note>A citation of Isaiah 57:2, a standard verse for funerals and headstones</note>. He descended to this lower world and merited a long life.
<margin>Amen, Selah</margin>
May the merit of his ancestors protect him and his offspring forever, Amen, Selah. Lest the nations say, "Where is their God?" <note>A plea from Psalm 79:10 for God to vindicate His people</note>. They shall come in the name of the LORD. My help comes from the LORD, Maker of heaven and earth <note>Psalm 121:2</note>.

<meta>The previous page concluded a eulogy for Moshe Ibn Yahya from 1656. This page shifts from mourning to ethical and mystical instruction (Musar and Kabbalah). It transitions from the historical record of a death into a treatise on "The Conduct of the Household," exploring the mystical foundations of marriage and family life through the lens of Rabbinic tradition.</meta>

The matter of what arises in the heart or the intellect regarding its conceptualization: for the matter of the king, his ministers, and all that they possess is a matter of established convention <note>original: "הניחו המונח" (hiniḥu ha-munaḥ) — a philosophical term referring to axioms or established premises</note>. And this applies to every single thing that is not according to law and justice; it is truly the Name <note>Refers to the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God</note>. This is also true in physical matters and all the occurrences that happen to them.

And behold, this matter is hinted at in the **Secret of the Chariot** <term>Sod HaMerkavah: An early form of Jewish mysticism focused on the vision of God's celestial throne-chariot as described by the prophet Ezekiel</term> in the Torah portion of *Yitro* <note>Exodus 18:1–20:23</note> and the portion of *Mishpatim* <note>Exodus 21:1–24:18</note>, as well as in the *Midrash Rabbah*, *Lamentations Rabbah*, and the *Yalkut* <note>Classic collections of Rabbinic homilies and legal interpretations</note>. 

And so, we are now moved in our hearts to explain some matters of the secrets of the Torah which we have received from our Sages, of blessed memory. And behold, it is as follows: for this matter is the great and awesome matter, the likes of which has not come before. And now we shall come to explain this matter at length, with the help of God, may He be blessed and may His mention be exalted forever.

---

After this brief introductory discourse, I saw fit to explain the matter of the **Conduct of the Household**. Behold, the foundation of the house is the man and his spouse. As we have established above, there is the matter of peace and love between a man and his wife. Truly, in this "house," there are great secrets and precious hints. Our Sages, of blessed memory, said: "If a man and woman merit it, the Divine Presence <term>Shekhinah: The feminine aspect of God's presence that dwells within the world</term> rests between them." 

Truly, this matter is hinted at in the secret of the Name *Yah* <note>The two-letter name of God, Yod (י) and Heh (ה)</note>. For the "Man" (*Ish*) contains the letter *Yod*, and the "Woman" (*Isha*) contains the letter *Heh*. If, Heaven forbid, the Divine Presence departs from between them, there remains only "Fire" and "Fire" <note>The Hebrew word for Man is איש (I-Y-Sh) and Woman is אשה (I-Sh-H). If you remove the letters of God’s name (י from Ish and ה from Isha), both words are reduced to אש (Esh), which means "fire." This is a classic Rabbinic wordplay suggesting that without God/Peace, a marriage becomes a consuming fire.</note>. This is the essence of the disputes and the quarreling that sometimes enters the home because of the **Evil Inclination** <note>original: "Yezter Ha-Ra" — the innate human impulse toward selfishness or destruction</note>, which acts as an accuser and an adversary. 

Therefore, it is proper for a person to be very, very careful in this matter and to pursue peace with all his strength. As our Sages, of blessed memory, said: "Great is peace, for it was given to the living," etc. Indeed, this matter is the key to all blessings and successes. For in a place where there is peace, blessing is found; but in a place where there is, Heaven forbid, conflict, there the Satan dances <note>A metaphorical expression for chaos and misfortune</note> and every evil thing is found. Our Sages, of blessed memory, warned of this in many places and told us many stories regarding the matter of peace and how it sustains the world. About this they said that the entire world only stands upon peace.

And now we shall come to explain the matter of the **Conduct of Sons and Daughters**, and how it is proper for a person to guide them in the straight path and in the path of the Torah and the commandments. For they are the roots from which the "good fruits" will emerge in the time to come. It is proper for a person to pray always for his children and his grandchildren, that they may be engaged in the Torah and the commandments for their own sake <note>original: "Lishmah" — performing a deed out of love for the act itself rather than for a reward</note> and that they should be God-fearing. By this, he will merit to see children and grandchildren engaged in the Torah and the commandments, and his portion will be with the righteous in Paradise. Amen, Selah.

<meta>The previous page transitioned from a 1656 eulogy into a mystical and ethical discourse regarding the "Conduct of the Household." This page continues that intellectual trajectory, moving into a defense of traditional rabbinic authority against philosophical critiques, specifically concerning the nature of creation and the properties of light.</meta>

Truly: it is fitting for every student of the Torah and every man in whom the spirit of the Lord resides to be careful in this matter, God forbid, lest he sin and bear guilt before the great and awesome word of the Lord which is before him; for all His words are truth and justice. If one should sin and act as a stranger, he shall not escape, and the Day of Judgment shall come for all who transgress. Behold, their iniquity shall be revealed, as it is written: "On the day I visit, I will visit their sin upon them" <note>Exodus 32:34</note>.

And behold, regarding the explanation of these laws, if the philosophers should say there is a limit <note>original: תכלית (tachlit) — can mean either a physical boundary or a purposeful end</note> to the matter—and indeed, in the explanation of these laws, if the philosophers say there is a limit to the matter—anyone who busies himself with this subject ought to be proficient in all the details and in every supreme discourse. We shall now explain them with the help of God.

And it happened one day, as I desired, that I saw in the book **Way of Faith** <term>Derech Emunah: A 15th-century philosophical and kabbalistic work by Meir ibn Gabbai, though several books share this title</term> by the learned Rabbi. He wrote there on page 87, and these are his words: "And as for what the later scholars questioned regarding this, it is not correct for them to say that the indivisible part <term>The "indivisible part" refers to the concept of the atom in medieval philosophy—the smallest possible unit of matter that cannot be split</term> shall perish; and one must not say that God, may He be blessed, will find for it another place or cause." This is what he wrote in Chapter 3 on page 148.

And behold, a certain man arose named Rabbi A. and spoke against his words, and this occurred in recent times. He wrote on the aforementioned page regarding the Rabbi’s words: "All his words are words of wind <note>meaning: nonsensical or empty breath</note> and have no substance." He further added in Chapter 3 on page 146, saying: "It is worthy to wonder at the words of the aforementioned Rabbi; I have already raised difficulties against his words in another place. Would that I knew his place so I might find him to speak my words before him." Therefore, I say, let the reader not believe the words of that man, and let him not harbor thoughts against the Rabbi's words, for the Rabbi’s words are truth, and that man's words are not words of truth, God forbid. I shall now explain the words of the Rabbi, of blessed and holy memory, in this place on the aforementioned page, and anyone who delves into his words will see that his mouth has spoken truth. The same applies to all that was mentioned above.

---

Some have explained that the Lord placed it in the heart of the scripture where it says, "And God said... let there be a separation." The holy Rabbi, etc., wrote in the book **Generations of Isaac** <term>Toldot Yitzchak: A popular Torah commentary by Isaac Aboab II, a 15th-century Spanish scholar known as the "Last Gaon of Castile"</term> by Rabbi Isaac Aboab, of blessed memory, as follows: Regarding what our Sages of blessed memory said in the Midrash <note>Rabbinic homiletic teachings</note> in Chapter 7: "Let everything be done with knowledge; He who sits in the heavens laughs at them," etc. We find that in the Torah, in one place it is written: "And God said, 'Let there be light,' and there was light" <note>Genesis 1:3</note>. Behold, at the end of the verse it is written "and there was light," whereas at the beginning of his words it is written "And God said, 'Let there be light.'" Why was it not said according to the usual language of the Scripture, "and it was so"? <note>The author notes that usually creation commands end with "and it was so" (vayehi ken), but here the word "light" is repeated.</note> To this they answered that the light was shining and radiating so intensely.

<margin>The scholars of the generation</margin>

And thirdly, we desire to address this in our words... and one must also wonder according to the words of the Midrash mentioned above, which wrote that on the first day the light was created, and it is the light about which it is written: "And God saw the light, that it was good" <note>Genesis 1:4</note>, and He hid it away for the righteous in the future to come. Regarding this, they asked: why were the two Great Luminaries <note>The sun and the moon</note> not created on that same day? At the end of the discussion, he wrote his words clearly in the name of Rabbi S. ben Ch. <note>likely Rabbi Solomon ben Hanan or a similar contemporary authority</note>, of blessed memory. He writes in Chapter 21 on page 98: "It is proper to understand the words of our Sages of blessed memory regarding the matter of the luminaries which the Holy One, Blessed be He, created on the fourth day. They appear in the form of small lights to the eye, but in truth, they are very great. The Lord has shown us His glory and His greatness; may the pleasantness of the Lord be upon us, and may all those who are wise shine like the brightness of the firmament" <note>Daniel 12:3</note>. These are the words of the learned Rabbi, of blessed memory.

May the Holy One, Blessed be He, help us for the sake of His glorious Name, and give us a heart to hear and understand the words of His Torah. Amen, may it be His will.

<meta>Continuity with the previous page: The previous text was a philosophical inquiry into atomism and divine creation. This page shifts abruptly into a homiletic or personal notebook style, opening with a wedding blessing for a friend named Eleazar, followed by various Torah commentaries (Midrash) on themes of sin, completeness, and ritual laws. This suggests the manuscript is a personal commonplace book or a collection of "delightful portions" (sermons/essays) compiled by the author, Yehudah son of Moshe.</meta>

->### Delightful Portions<-
->**On the wedding days of my beloved friend Eleazar**<-

**Delightful Portions** on the days of the wedding of my beloved and dear friend Eleazar. May joy and gladness be found therein, and may sorrow and sighing flee away. And may Eleazar rejoice with his wife, finding joy in her and the voice of thanksgiving. 

The <term>Sages of Blessed Memory</term> <note>original: חז"ל (Hazal) — an acronym for the "Sages of Blessed Memory," the foundational rabbis of the Talmud</note> said in the tractate of **Nedarim** <note>Vows</note>: "If we had not sinned, only the five books of the Torah and the Book of Joshua would have been given to us." However, the <term>Writings</term> <note>original: הכתובים (Ketuvim) — the third section of the Hebrew Bible, including Psalms, Proverbs, etc.</note> were added because we sinned; sin caused the addition of these books, and they have become a burden for us.

***

And it is further asked regarding what the Sages explained about the verse, "And the anger of the Lord was kindled against Israel, and He sold them into the hand of **Kushan Rishathaim**" <note>A king of Aram mentioned in the Book of Judges; his name "Rishathaim" literally means "Double-Wickedness"</note>. They say he committed two wicked acts: one in the days of Kushan and one in the days of Goliath and in the days of Chaza. It is asked: How do we know to say he was present at that time, and from whom did we learn this? They said it was because he possessed "double wickedness" that he was called Kushan Rishathaim. 

And in the portion of **Vayishlach** <note>The Torah portion "And he sent," Genesis 32:4–36:43</note>, the scripture says: "And Jacob sent messengers before him to Esau his brother to the land of Seir, the field of Edom." **Rashi** <note>Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval commentator</note> explains that he did this because Esau was destined to settle in the land of Edom.

***

<margin>
A
</margin>

It is somewhat difficult: If you say, "Why do we need the scripture to say 'and both of them as one'?" how would we know this? For when Rabbi Eliezer began, he said if the scripture is required at that time, as **Rashi** explains, it refers to one who "slights the washing of hands." This means when he eats "morning bread" <note>original: פת שחרית (pat shacharit) — breakfast, considered by the Sages to have medicinal and spiritual benefits</note> and eats... In the **Midrash Tanhuma** <note>A collection of homiletic teachings on the Torah</note> on the portion of **Vayishlach**, Rabbi Judah the Levite says: "Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, when I will send a hunger into the land; not a hunger for bread, nor a thirst for water, but for hearing the words of the Lord." The Sages wrote that when the Holy One, Blessed be He, spoke with the prophets in prophecy, they spoke to Him in matters of Law and Judgment; they were not wicked, yet they turned their backs to everything and their faces toward the West. So it is written in the precious book regarding the "morning watch," that He gave them bread and water.

***

It is further asked regarding the verse: "And Jacob arrived **Shalem** <note>Whole, complete, or peaceful</note> to the city of Shechem." **Rashi** explains that he was not lacking in his soul: he was **whole in his body** (having been healed from his limp), **whole in his wealth** (having lost nothing despite the massive gift he sent to Esau), and **whole in his Torah** (having not forgotten his learning while in the house of Laban the Aramean). What does Rashi mean to clarify by saying "whole in the city of Shechem"? For it is because of these three things that he is called "Shalem" in the scripture. Rashi explains "he was not lacking in his soul" because he was not a disciple of Esau, God forbid; do not say that his heart followed the wickedness of Esau, for the prophet called him "whole" (Shalem). Furthermore, regarding being "whole in his wealth" (not lacking because of the gift), why was it necessary for the scripture to tell us this? It must be that everything came to him by right of Law.

***

It is further asked how **Rashi** explained in the portion of **Ki Tetze** <note>The Torah portion "When you go out," Deuteronomy 21:10–25:19</note>: "When you build a new house, you shall make a **parapet** <note>original: מעקה (ma’akeh) — a safety railing on a roof</note>." The law of the parapet and the custom of the house is as the Master said: it is also a warning for your houses to keep the commandments of the King. Rabbi Joshua ben Levi interpreted this and added to it: what is the parapet for which one is liable? Why does it require three stones or a purchase? The Rabbis said it is "lest the faller fall from it." This is what King Solomon said in **Ecclesiastes**: "All is vanity and a pursuit of wind." Every man is obligated to make a "parapet" for himself—to be whole in his wealth and his soul. From this law of the house, every man is obligated to make a "parapet" for the Lord and to ensure his house is whole and free from all sin and wickedness.

***

From me, the lowly writer, Yehudah son of the honorable Moshe, may his memory be for a blessing in the World to Come.

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: The previous page concluded a series of homiletic reflections or "Delightful Portions" by Yehudah son of Moshe, focusing on the biblical story of Kushan Rishathaim. This page shifts into a more technical Kabbalistic discourse, likely influenced by the teachings of Isaac Luria (the Ari), focusing on the mystical structure of the soul and the movement of the Divine Presence (Malchut) during the Sabbath and prayer.</meta>

Know that all that is written in this matter concerning the five names of the **soul** <note>original: ה' שמות של הנשמה — referring to the five levels of the soul in Jewish mysticism: Nefesh (vitality), Ruach (spirit), Neshamah (breath), Chayah (living one), and Yechidah (singular unity)</note> and similar topics, is all contained within the **mystery of the Kingdom** <term>Malchut: the lowest of the ten Sefirot or divine emanations, representing the Divine Presence or the feminine aspect of God in the physical world</term>. 

Behold, it is known that on the **Sabbath day**, the *Malchut* ascends until it reaches the level of **Understanding** <term>Binah: the third Sefirah, associated with the divine womb or mother</term> and other such levels. This change occurs according to the specific state in which we find ourselves. For it is known that on the Sabbath, the *Malchut* rises to *Binah*. Everything we explain regarding the *Malchut* on the Sabbath refers to that specific time when she ascends there.

However, it is known that within the Sabbath day itself, there are **three distinct stages** of ascent:
1. On **Friday evening** (Sabbath Eve), the *Malchut* ascends to the level of the **Foundation** <term>Yesod</term> of the **Lesser Countenance** <term>Zeir Anpin (Z"A): the masculine aspect of the divine Sefirot</term>.
2. In the **morning**, she ascends as far as **Understanding** (*Binah*).
3. At the **afternoon service** <note>Mincha</note>, she ascends to the **Crown** <term>Keter: the highest and most primary of the Sefirot</term>, and so on.

Indeed, everything we have explained in this matter is according to the status of the *Malchut* in relation to the other <term>Sefirot</term> <note>divine attributes or emanations</note>.

-> בסוד התפלה <-
->**In the Mystery of Prayer**<-

### First Commandment: Concerning Prayer and the Shema
It is known that anyone who focuses their **intention** <note>Kavanah: the meditative mindset required for prayer</note> during prayer and the **Recitation of the Shema** <note>the central declaration of Jewish faith</note> as is proper, repairs their vital soul, their spirit, and their higher soul. They raise them up through the mystery of **Feminine Waters** <term>M"N (Mayin Nukvin): a mystical concept referring to the spiritual arousal or initiative sent from humanity below to the Divine above</term>, as is known from the secrets of prayer. In every single prayer, there is an aspect of an **additional soul** <note>Neshama Yeteira</note>, and a person must intend to raise their soul upward into the mystery of the *Malchut*.

### Second Commandment: Concerning Blessings
It is known that all **blessings** are intended to draw down **divine abundance** <term>Shefa: the flow of divine energy or blessing</term> from the upper world to the lower world, specifically into the *Malchut*, which contains all blessings. One must intend in every single blessing to draw that abundance down from its **Supernal Source**.

### Third Commandment
It is known that all **commandments** <note>Mitzvot</note> are intended to repair the various worlds through the mystery of the *Malchut*, etc.

<summary>This page explains the Lurianic Kabbalistic concept of the soul's ascent and the movement of the Sefirah Malchut (the Divine Presence) through different levels of the godhead during the Sabbath and through the performance of commandments, prayer, and blessings.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, Malchut, Sefirot, Sabbath, Prayer, Shema, Mitzvot, Divine Abundance (Shefa), Feminine Waters (M"N), Soul (Neshama)</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: The previous page discussed the mystical ascent of the soul and the Sefirah of Malchut (Kingdom) during the Sabbath, specifically how the Divine Presence rises through the levels of the spiritual worlds. This page expands on that theme by distinguishing between simple faith and the deeper, intellectual "knowledge" required to achieve true mystical union with the Divine.</meta>

A bit of the reasoning mentioned above regarding the speech we are discussing, and this view is explained in its proper place.

However, it is proper to know what we have previously established: that whoever is awakened by these matters to become a believer in the Torah of our God, His judgments, and His ways—and to know how one may be pleasing before the Omnipresent (blessed be He) and merit the **Hidden Good** <note>original: הטוב הצפון — a reference to the reward stored for the righteous in the afterlife</note>—will indeed merit that Hidden Good according to the level of faith they hold. This is the virtue of **Faith** <term>Emunah: often interpreted in Kabbalah not just as belief, but as a vessel for receiving divine light</term>. Anyone who believes with perfect faith, even if they are among the common people who lack the intellectual capacity for a deep knowledge of the Creator (blessed be He), will nevertheless merit a high level of reward by virtue of their simple and innocent faith.

<margin>Part</margin> As our Sages (of blessed memory) said: "All Israel have a **part** in the World to Come." They explained that this means even those who have not reached the level of perfection in intellect or in the knowledge of the Creator (blessed be He) will nonetheless merit a level of spiritual reward because of their faith in the Torah of our God, the books of the Prophets, and the words of our Sages—as is known among the **Sages of Truth** <term>Chachmei HaEmet: a common traditional term for Kabbalists</term>. 

Yet, there is a great distinction between the level of reward for such a person and the level of one who is a **Maskil** <note>An "intellectual" or "enlightened one" who possesses deep mystical insight</note>, who clearly understands the order of the **Supernal Governance** and the power of the **Sefirot** <term>The ten divine emanations or attributes through which God interacts with the world</term>. Such a person understands how they are unified in their Source, the levels through which they spread forth, the aspects by which they act in every single world, and how they oversee all of existence according to its needs. Behold, this level is exceedingly superior and much greater than the reward of one who possesses simple faith alone. For the one who understands these things will merit to **cleave** <term>Devekut: the mystical state of "clinging" or "attachment" to the Divine</term> to the upper levels and to truly enjoy the **Radiance of the Divine Presence** <term>Ziv HaShekhinah</term>. The more one understands and knows these supernal matters, the more they will merit to be elevated.

<margin>Study</margin> As our Sages said: "From this we infer that there is **Study** <note>original: עיון — intensive intellectual or meditative investigation</note> etc." The Sages of Truth explained this to mean that anyone who delves deeply into this study to understand and contemplate the order of Governance in its sources will, by virtue of that understanding, merit the elevation of their soul to that very Source which they are contemplating. They will merit to enjoy the radiance of that level. Regarding this, the verse says about <gloss>Our God</gloss> (blessed be His name) in the **Song of Songs**: "Return, return, O Shulammite; return, return, that we may look upon you" <note>Song of Songs 7:1</note>. Our Sages interpreted this to mean that the **Shekhinah** <term>The Divine Presence, the feminine aspect of God</term> speaks to Israel, as is found in the Midrash Rabbah. 

Following this, the matter of this knowledge of the levels and the **Secret of Governance** will be further explained—specifically what it effects in the soul of the seeker, and how through this, one merits to reach the level of **Supernal Holiness**, of which it is said: "Sanctify yourselves and you shall be holy." 

This matter will be explained in the coming chapters, God willing. Now, let us return to our subject: anyone who is a *Maskil* and knows the supernal levels—how the flow of the upper sources emanates, governs, and influences all of existence in general and Israel in particular, and how it spreads through all created beings according to their temperament and preparation—will, by virtue of this knowledge, merit the elevation of the soul to the Supernal Source from which it emerged. There, the soul will remain attached in eternal life. This is the secret of what is written: "And to Him you shall **cleave**." 

Concerning this, our Sages said in the Midrash: "And this is the **Faith**." This refers to how our father Abraham (peace be upon him) believed in the existence of the Creator and the order of His governance and providence. Through this, he merited a great and lofty level. Likewise, anyone who walks in these paths of his will merit the level of **True Perfection** hidden for the righteous in the future to come. 

What must further be known is the secret of how this knowledge acts upon the soul of the seeker and the benefit derived from it. For knowledge and understanding are not things external to the soul; rather, they are the very reality of the soul itself. For the soul is the "knower," the "known," and the "knowledge" itself, as the great **Philosophers** <note>original: חכמי המחקר — literally "Sages of Research," usually referring to medieval Jewish Aristotelians like Maimonides</note> have written. In particular, the Sages of Truth have agreed that the soul and the intellect are one and the same. Therefore, when the soul understands divine matters, it becomes divine itself and cleaves to the supernal sources according to the level of its understanding. This is the greatest and most supreme level of all.

<vocab>Faith (Emunah), Sages of Truth (Kabbalists), Divine Emanations (Sefirot), Divine Presence (Shekhinah), Contemplation/Study (Iyyun), Cleaving (Devekut), Spiritual Reward, The World to Come</vocab>

<summary>This page distinguishes between simple faith, which grants every person a share in the afterlife, and the superior path of mystical knowledge (Gnosis). It explains that by intellectually and spiritually understanding the Sefirot and divine governance, the soul becomes one with that divine knowledge and achieves true union with God.</summary>

<meta>Continuity with previous page: The text continues a discussion on the levels of faith, moving from the simple belief of the common person to the deeper, mystical understanding of how Divine Providence operates even within obstacles and seemingly negative events.</meta>

This is also the principle regarding everything a person perceives in the world: it is all from Him, blessed be He. This is especially true of a religious duty or a situation that appears to be contrary to the will of God; in truth, everything is from Him, blessed be He, through **Divine Providence** <term>Hashgacha Pratit: the belief that God actively oversees and governs every detail of the individual's life</term> specifically directed at him. This follows what the **Holy Maggid** <note>original: המגיד הקדש — likely referring to Rabbi Dov Ber of Mezeritch, a central figure in early Hasidism</note> (of blessed memory) wrote regarding **Fallen Fear** <term>Yirah Nefulah: a concept where one’s fear is misplaced on worldly things rather than on God; the spiritual task is to "elevate" that fear back to its divine source</term>, which one must elevate to its root, as mentioned. This is the essence of Divine Providence. In truth, because everything is from Him, all evil and all obstacles are naturally nullified. For once a person knows that everything is from Him, may He be blessed, and when he is privileged to understand this, then every obstacle and every

<margin>Note</margin>
evil is nullified, and everything remains holy to the Lord. Through this, we can explain several passages in the Talmud and Midrash: that "everything goes to one place" and "at the root, everything is one." Even in those matters that appear to go against His will, it is actually His will that this obstacle exists so that a person might **break his heart** <note>original: ישבר האדם את לבו — a state of humility and contrition necessary for spiritual growth</note> and similar reactions,

<margin>Note</margin>
thereby arriving at an even greater spiritual fervor. This is, in brief, the matter of Divine Providence and the nullification of obstacles through this knowledge. Examine this well.

***

This is also the meaning of what is told in the **Gemara** <note>The component of the Talmud comprising rabbinical analysis and commentary</note> concerning the story of **Rabbi Akiva** <note>A famous 1st-century sage</note>, who was traveling on the road and had with him a donkey, a lamp, and a rooster. [When his animals died and his lamp went out], he said: **"Whatever the Merciful One does, He does for the best"** <note>original: כל מה דעביד רחמנא לטב עביד — a classic Aramaic expression of absolute faith in God's goodness</note>. At first glance, this is difficult: since everything is from Him, what is the meaning of "He does for the best"? Is not the obstacle itself also from Him? Rather, the explanation is that the obstacle itself is a great good, as is known from the story there. This is also the meaning of "all is for the good," even the obstacles that seemingly oppose His will—

<margin>Note</margin>
they are, in truth, His inner will. This is also the theme of the Torah portion **Vayetze** <note>Genesis 28:10</note>: "And Jacob went out from Beersheba and went toward Haran. And he encountered a place..." The obstacle was that he had no place to lodge, but in truth, this happened so he would merit the **Vision of the Ladder** <note>Jacob’s dream of angels ascending and descending a ladder</note>. Understand this.

Similarly, "everything goes to one place," for everything is His will. In every single thing, there is a **Holy Spark** <term>Nitzotz Kadosh: in Kabbalah, these are fragments of divine light trapped within the material world that must be liberated through holy acts</term> that gives life to that thing. A person must elevate that spark to its source, as is known from the writings of the **Ari** <note>Rabbi Isaac Luria (1534–1572), the father of modern Kabbalah</note> (of blessed memory). Everything returns to one place, as stated above. Contemplate and understand this.

<summary>This page explains the Hasidic concept of Divine Providence, arguing that even obstacles and "evil" are manifestations of God's will intended to spark spiritual growth. It illustrates this through the stories of Rabbi Akiva's optimism and Jacob's ladder, concluding with the Kabbalistic duty to elevate holy sparks from the material world.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Providence, Hashgacha Pratit, Rabbi Akiva, Holy Sparks, Nitzotzot, Fallen Fear, Maggid of Mezeritch, Jacob's Ladder, obstacles, nullification of evil</keywords>

For God is the one who leads you—may His name be blessed and His memory endure forever and ever, Amen.

<margin>the city of Mszanów</margin> Mszanów, to the holy city of **Safed** <note>original: צפת תובב"א — "May it be rebuilt and established quickly in our days, Amen"</note>, and the Lord, may He be blessed, led us beside still waters upon the sea. ### An Account of What Occurred

An account of what happened is as follows: while we were on the journey during the days of **Purim** in the year [5]433 <note>original: תל"ג — 1673 CE</note>, we were walking with one large caravan, and our leader was **Azman Aga** from the holy city of Safed. We went from there from Amus <note>possibly Ma'amusa or a local waypoint</note> and went to a place called **Gidida** by carriage, for that is the way they travel there; whoever does not have a carriage travels on foot. There were about thirty Jewish men, and also women and children with us. On Thursday, the 25th of the month of **Adar**, two days before the New Moon of Nisan, we passed through a small city called **Al-Fua**, and we had a long stop there, being delayed for nearly half the day.

And afterward, on the eve of the Holy Sabbath, the 26th of the aforementioned month, we came to a place called **Jenin**. There was a larger stop there than for the rest of the caravan, and we were among them. There, on the eve of the Sabbath, two hours before dark, we washed our hands and faces in honor of the Holy Sabbath, as is always our custom on Sabbath eve.
<margin>17th of the month</margin> We were delayed there the entire Holy Sabbath day. On Sunday, the 17th of the aforementioned month <note>There is a discrepancy here; if the previous Friday was the 26th of Adar, this Sunday would likely be the 28th of Adar or 1st of Nisan. The author may be using a different dating system or miscounting</note>, we rose in the morning with the aforementioned caravan. We traveled that whole day and came on Sunday evening to the place called **Ein Ganim**, and there too was a long stop throughout that night. In the morning we rose to travel on Monday,
<margin>18th of the month</margin> the 18th of the aforementioned month. We went with the caravan until we were near the city called **Shechem** <note>Nablus</note>.
<margin>There we were on the eve of the Holy Sabbath</margin> The rest of the caravan went on Friday and reached Shechem, but there they left us almost alone. 

When it was the morning of the aforementioned Monday, before sunrise, the aforementioned **Azman Aga** stood over us, our donkeys, and our camels, and went before us with his aforementioned servants and all of his baggage. In the meantime, on that day before midday, about thirty men reached us—wild, evil, and very sinful people <note>Bandits or local marauders</note>. All of these people came upon us with great force and wanted to rob us of everything we had with us. They attacked us and beat us with very great blows, and we were in great danger. 

By the hand of God, we had a great rescue through the aforementioned Azman Aga and those who were with us, and also with the help of the Lord, may He be blessed. In the midst of the heavy blows, they killed five of the men from the wicked Arabs <note>original: הערבי' הרשעים — likely referring to the nomadic or local bandits</note>, and from the Israelites, one man was killed—his name was **Rabbi Chaim**, son of Rabbi Shmuel, may his memory be for a blessing. The rest of the people were saved. On the third day <note>Tuesday</note>, we came to the place called **Lubban**, and there too was a long stop; we arrived there in great peace. We traveled on the fourth day,
<margin>19th of the month</margin> which was the 19th day of the aforementioned month, until on the evening of the 19th, we stood all night outside the holy city of **Jerusalem**. On Thursday morning, the 20th of the aforementioned month, we entered in great peace into Jerusalem, the holy city, may it be rebuilt and established quickly in our days, Amen.

<meta>This page transitions from the travel diary of the previous page into a homiletic (sermon-like) discourse. The author uses the legal language of the Torah and the Talmud to reflect on the nature of sin, atonement, and the "redemption of the soul." The tone shifts from historical recording to ethical and mystical interpretation, likely written during the same period in the late 17th century.</meta>

One is obligated to pay the price of its sale. As it is written: "And if any of the flesh of the sacrifice of the peace offerings <note>original: זבח השלמים — *Zevach HaShelamim*; a type of voluntary offering in the Temple expressing well-being</note> be eaten at all on the third day... it shall not be accepted for the one who offers it" <note>Leviticus 7:18</note>. After the sale, it shall be burned in fire, etc. He [the commentator] was not precise, but intended to say that one is obligated to give the price of the sale, which is the redemption <note>original: פדיין — *Pidyon*; a ransom or redemption price</note>, etc., until the sale serves as an atonement and redemption for his own soul, etc. And even though it is not written "why he sold," etc., it should have been written "and it shall atone for him," etc. We can say in a general way that if one has sinned, he is obligated to give the price of atonement. And this is what the verse means:
"A sin! When a person shall sin," etc. Rather, the scripture wants to clarify for us from the text that his price is not within his own soul once he has sinned, and his angel cannot act as an atonement for him unless an atonement rises up for him. And this is what is meant by "the sale shall be eaten"—the interpretation precisely links "the sale" to mean the redemption. It is the utterance of his mouth; if he is the one who offers it, perhaps there will be no atonement. And in this way, it also works well with the phrase "it shall be an abomination" <note>original: פגול — *Piggul*; a technical term for a sacrifice disqualified by the improper intentions of the priest</note>, meaning it shall be flawed for him, according to the explanation. For he knows that it is but a small part of the sale and the redemption. Thus far are his words.

Furthermore, it is written in the Talmud <note>original: ש"ס — *Shas*; the Six Orders of the Mishnah/Talmud</note> at the beginning of Tractate **Shabbat**: "The departures of the Sabbath [are two...]" <note>The opening Mishnah of Tractate Shabbat discusses the laws of carrying objects between domains</note>. Why did the author abbreviate here? Rabbi [Judah the Prince] said that he comes to say something simple: when there is a matter of "sale" or "redemption," it is appropriate to find merit for him so that he may be brought out <note>A play on words between "departures" and being brought out of sin or punishment</note>, lest he be cast out as a sinner. And this is what is found in the Talmud at the beginning of **Shabbat**: "The departures of the Sabbath," etc. The simple explanation is that it refers to the "strength of the decree," namely the redemption of the sale. The scripture hints that "departures" refers to redemption. This is what Rabbi meant by speaking simply—since we have a "departure," it is not just in a general sense, but a simple occurrence. He wanted to express the power of the deed. "And if the sale [is eaten] on the third day," etc., so too the sale follows the deed. The simple meaning of "on the third day it shall not be accepted" is what is found in the aforementioned Talmud: "The departures of the Sabbath are two." Study this well, for it is as explained above.

---

<margin>And further proof: the Midrash <note>Ancient homiletic interpretations of the Bible</note> says: The Holy One, Blessed be He, said to the children of men: "A single soul has sinned; its atonement is in its house and its redemption is by law." The same law applies to the living; that is, atonement is relevant. He said that this level... it is appropriate to write "And he shall give the redemption of his soul" <note>Exodus 30:12</note>. See the Midrash and study it well.</margin>
And while we are dealing with this portion, it is found in the Talmud, Tractate **Shabbat**, in the chapter "*HaBoneh*" <note>The Builder</note>, where Rabbi Zera said: "Four things... the Holy One, Blessed be He, did for the sake of the merit of Israel." Even those called "wicked in Israel" are included, for it is written: "And all your deeds are written in a book" <note>Ethics of the Fathers 2:1</note>. Study this well. This is what is meant by the Talmud and what is meant by "the law" in the Midrash, and it fits well with the aforementioned passage in Tractate **Shabbat**. See the manuscript there and see the *Tikunim* <note>Likely referring to the *Tikunei Zohar*, a mystical text</note>. The explanation of "The departures of the Sabbath are two" is that the "two" refers to the redemption and the sale, as mentioned above. It seems to me his words should be explained like the previous interpretation: the [first] "departure of the Sabbath" is the sale, and the second is the atonement itself, which is the redemption of his soul. Study this well. If a man sins, etc., and must give the price of the sale, the deed is relevant. The language of the Talmud is very precise in saying "two," for "departures of the Sabbath" means, as explained, departures from suffering and from the resulting curse, etc. This is also precise in the beginning of Tractate **Shabbat**. It is simple: since he sinned, he is obligated to give the price of the sale and "give the ransom for his soul." Study this well. That which the Talmud says—"two"—is as explained: the first "departure" is the one mentioned, and the second is the redemption of his soul. Study this well.

<summary>This page presents a homiletic interpretation of sacrificial laws and Talmudic passages, equating the "departures" of the Sabbath with the soul's redemption from sin and suffering. The author argues that atonement requires a metaphorical "redemption price" or "sale" to release the individual from the consequences of their actions.</summary>
<keywords>Atonement, Redemption, Tractate Shabbat, Piggul, Peace Offerings, Soul's Ransom, Rabbi Zera, Midrash, Talmudic Allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the chronological and thematic analysis of biblical narratives. Isaac, the Holy One, blessed be He, spoke to him in a "double" manner <note>original: ב' ב' — likely referring to the repetition of names in divine calls, such as "Abraham, Abraham," signifying affection or urgency</note>, for it is written that in every place the language of "near" is used. And the Holy One, blessed be He, said to him: "Go forth..." <note>Genesis 12:1</note> and so on.

and afterward the angels came to Abraham with the news of the son. And afterward: "And Abraham was still standing before the Lord."
And afterward: "And they said to Abraham, 'Where is Sarah your wife?'" and so on. And afterward: "And it came to pass after these things,"
which was the trial of the <term>Binding of Isaac</term> <note>original: עקידה — *Akedah*; the biblical account of Abraham's near-sacrifice of his son Isaac</note>. "And it was after these things" that it was told to Abraham: "Behold, Milkah also has borne

to Abimelech," and so on. "And the Lord appeared to him and said: 'Do not go down to Egypt,'" and so on. "And Isaac sowed in that land
and found in that year" and so on. "And the man grew great and went forward" and so on. "And it came to pass when Isaac was old and his eyes were dimmed," and so on.
"And Esau was forty years old and he took a wife," and so on. "And they were a bitterness of spirit to Isaac," and so on. And afterward: "And it came to pass when Isaac was old
and his eyes were too dim to see," and so on, "and he called Esau," and so on. And afterward: "And Esau went to Ishmael and took Mahalath," and so on.
And the <term>Ramban</term> <note>original: רמב"ן — Rabbi Moses ben Nahman, a 13th-century Spanish scholar and mystic</note>, of blessed memory, wrote regarding the prophecy of Isaac to Esau, and so on.

-> 37 <-

Pharaoh's dream, in which he saw two dreams: the dream of the cows and the dream of the stalks. In my opinion, the dream of the cows
alluded to the seven years of plenty, and the dream of the stalks alluded to the seven years of famine, because the stalks
look hungrier than the cows. But the cows were "handsome in appearance and fat in flesh" because the cows
are the source of human life, while the stalks are the food itself. Therefore, He showed him the cows first and afterward
the stalks, to teach that the cows—which are the source and the essence—are the first seven years,
and the stalks—which are secondary—are the last seven years. This is why Joseph said: "Pharaoh's dream is one,"
meaning that the two dreams are one matter. To this he said: "Behold, seven years are coming..." and afterward: "and seven years of famine will arise after them," and so on.
And he said regarding the doubling of the dream to Pharaoh twice, "for the matter is established by God," and so on.
And this is the explanation of the verse: "And behold, Pharaoh was dreaming."

-> 38 <-

The staff that turned into a serpent: "and it became a serpent, and Moses fled from before it," and so on. "And the Lord said to Moses: 'Stretch out your hand
<note>Line crossed out: and grasp its tail; and he stretched out his hand and caught it, and it became a staff in his palm, etc.</note>
and grasp its tail; and he stretched out his hand and caught it, and it became a staff in his palm," and so on.
And this is the explanation of the scripture: "and it became a serpent, and Moses fled from before it," etc., because Moses, our teacher—peace be upon him—saw in this an allusion
to the wicked Pharaoh, who is the "fleeting serpent," as it is written: "The great dragon <note>original: תנין — *Tanin*; can mean serpent, dragon, or sea-monster</note> crouching in the midst of his rivers."
And Moses saw in this that he was destined to triumph over Pharaoh and subdue him under him. Therefore, he "stretched out his hand
and grasped its tail," for the tail is the end of the matter and the point of surrender. This is what the scripture means: "and it became a staff
in his palm," to say that the power and dominion would be in the hand of Moses, peace be upon him. Therefore, at the stand of the Burning Bush,
this was said to Moses to strengthen his heart when he came to Pharaoh to take the Children of Israel out of Egypt.

to teach that the hand of holiness has the upper hand, and that the Holy One, blessed be He, will fight against the Egyptians and save Israel
from the hand of their oppressors with great strength and an outstretched arm. And this is what the scripture says: "with an outstretched arm
and with great judgments," etc.

<meta>This page transitions from the midrashic and chronological accounts of the Patriarchs (Abraham and Isaac) found on the previous page into a mystical and philosophical discourse. It explores the internal psychological process of thought and its connection to the Ten Sefirot (divine emanations), citing a "wise man from Turkey" and referencing the Sefer Yetzirah (Book of Creation).</meta>

I found and saw this wonderful matter regarding the **Ten Songs** <note>original: עשר שירות — possibly a poetic reference to the Ten Sefirot or a specific mystical liturgy</note> which were found written by a certain sage in the **land of Turkey** and the lands of the East, and whose great name is **So-and-so** <note>original: פלוני (*Ploni*) — a placeholder used when a name is unknown or being withheld</note>. Know that the tenth is the "first examination," which reminds and urges a person regarding all his actions and deeds before they reach the brain, in the sense that he should supervise them while they are still in the "power of thought" <note>the potential stage of an idea</note> before they emerge into action. And behold, the order is: **Thought, Intellect, Understanding, and Knowledge** <note>original: מחשבה, שכל, בינה, דעת (*Machshavah, Sekhel, Binah, Da'at*) — these represent the cognitive faculties in Jewish mystical psychology</note>.

And behold, all deeds are equal and cannot be weighed in the "scales of the balance of justice." I heard further regarding this "examination" that the fruits of thought may be **dry or moist** and prepared; and from the expressions of speech, through letters and through **Understanding** (*Binah*), they change, and from there bad things may emerge. It is known that in the prison house one finds many of the imprisoned—this one for a "serpent" and that one for "sorcery"—and in the prison house many and mighty ones were found who were not known for what they were. 

And there is one who thought a "good thought" but did not succeed in it, yet it was a "thought for the sake of Heaven" and the **Holy One, blessed be He, joins it to the deed** <note>A famous Rabbinic principle: "A good thought is joined by God to an action," meaning one is rewarded for good intentions even if they cannot be fulfilled</note> and to the intention, and from it, a great and mighty reward is received. The same applies to those things whose fruits produced "fruits in a person" who is good and does good to all creatures and living beings. Furthermore, a few of these things were found which belong only to **unique individuals** <term>Chosen Virtuous Ones</term> <note>original: יחידי סגולה (*Yechidei Segulah*) — spiritually elite or saintly individuals</note>. 

He whose intention is desirable, whose thought and action are good, and all of whose deeds are for the sake of Heaven, shall not lack any good. Together they are written in the "scales of balance and weight." And study this well, for these matters require great contemplation; it is also proper to understand them well—whether they are "internal" or "external" to the person. I have explained this to know what the "Fathers" are who beget these "fruits" through the Lord. 

I heard and understood, and behold, there was a man [who was one] of the "fruits" who contemplated in his thought and imagined in his heart the likeness of these things—that they are like the likeness of the **souls that blow** in the world. I set my heart to all these, and this "examination" I found written in the <term>Book of Creation</term> <note>original: ספר הבריאה (*Sefer ha-Briah*) — likely referring to the *Sefer Yetzirah*, the foundational text of Jewish mysticism</note>. From the power of the "examination" we mentioned above, a person shall say: "Blessed be the name of the Creator." And behold, there are three "examinations" in this matter of which we spoke.

<note>The following section is crossed out with a large X in the manuscript.</note>

I found written further regarding this matter of the **Ten Sefirot of Nothingness** <note>original: עשר ספירות שבלימה (*Eser Sefirot She-belimah*) — the ten primary divine emanations or dimensions of reality</note>. Know that the **Intellect** is the root of all things, and it is called "Ten Sefirot," and this is the man, the intellect of a sage. Furthermore, I saw that this is written as an introduction to the **Secret of the Ten Sefirot** which are found within the intellect of a person. 

The "fourth examination" is that a person should say to himself: *"Hear, my son, your father's instruction, and do not forsake your mother's teaching"* <note>Proverbs 1:8</note>. This is the **First Sefirah** through which all of creation was created. Behold, the entire world is within it, standing and placed in the hand of this "mighty Intellect" above which there is nothing higher. I understood much in this matter. Every person should discern with the intellect they have merited from the mouth of the **Almighty** <note>original: מפי הגבורה (*Mipi ha-Gevurah*) — a traditional term for Divine revelation</note> to say from now on: that the Creator, blessed be His name, is **Wise, Understanding, and Speaking**, and His Intellect is His Wisdom.

A portion of what I wrote previously, by the grace of God, many days ago regarding this matter, according to my limited capacity at that time. And now we come to this attribute, for God’s kindness is upon it, and I shall say a certain portion of truth: for behold, it is written, "And behold, it was very good" <gloss>Genesis 1:31</gloss>.

"And behold, it was good"—this refers to the **Good Inclination** <note>the *Yetzer Tov*, the innate human drive toward moral and spiritual behavior</note>.
"Very good"—this refers to the **Evil Inclination** <note>the *Yetzer Hara*, the drive toward physical desires which, when channeled properly, leads to industry and procreation</note>.
"And behold, it was good"—this is the attribute of **Life**.
"Very good"—this is the attribute of **Death**.

"And behold, it was good"—this is **Paradise** <note>the Garden of Eden</note>.
"Very good"—this is **Gehenna** <note>the place of spiritual purgation or punishment</note>.
"And behold, it was good"—this is the attribute of **Reward**.
"Very good"—this is the attribute of **Punishment** <gloss>attributed to Samuel</gloss>.

But is the attribute of punishment actually 'good'? One might wonder! Rather, it is because it exacts justice for the blood of man <unclear>etc.</unclear>"

A certain portion of truth that is found in the Midrash: And the Sages say, "'And behold, it was very good'—this refers to the **Angel of Death**." For it is written: "And behold, it was very good. <unclear>And there was</unclear>
<margin>ibid.</margin>
evening and there was morning, the **sixth day**." The explanation is that once the sixth day arrived, in which **First** <gloss>Adam</gloss> was created and death was decreed for him, it was therefore said of that day "very good." 

Now, this is difficult: Is death actually good? Rather, it is because there is **Kindness** (*Chesed*) within it. For through death, a person's sin is atoned for, and he "returns to God." Behold, death is the "Complete Kindness" after which there is no more iniquity or sin. Therefore, it is said "And behold, it was very good" <unclear>precisely</unclear>. <meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on local customs and the authority of the Chida (Rabbi Hayyim Yosef David Azulai) into a profound philosophical inquiry. The author moves from legal precedents to the fundamental theological debate regarding the creation of the world, contrasting the Aristotelian view of an eternal universe with the Torah's account of creation in time.</meta>

<note>A series of diagonal lines cross through the first 14 lines of this text, likely indicating a draft to be revised or a section the author wished to reconsider.</note>

That is to say, and the understanding derived from it is that an acting agent functions in three ways. First, one who acts by virtue of its own essence for its own sake; this is the Creator, blessed be His name <note>original: *Yitbarach Shemo*</note>, who created everything for His own glory. It would be improper to say of Him that He was once inactive and then felt a "need" to act—asking why He did not create until that moment—because it is not in the nature of His existence to act at one time and not at another. Rather, the Creator, exalted and elevated, in whom there is no change, acts continuously at every moment and instant. From this, it would follow that from His own side, He is always acting. If so, the world would be eternal <term>eternal: *kadmon*, the philosophical view held by Aristotle that the universe has no beginning and has always existed</term>. Just as He acted at one moment, so He must have acted always. This, however, contains a contradiction to what is written in the Torah portion <gloss>of the one who says it was created anew</gloss> of **Genesis**, which states that the world was created anew at a specific time. The philosophers say that there is no such thing as "time" before the world and that it was always there, and that God was also always acting. However, according to the Truth, what is written in the Torah is the truth: the world was created anew <term>created anew: *nitchadesh*, the theological view that the world had a beginning in time</term>, and of this there is no doubt. The reason we cannot comprehend how He, blessed be He, could act at one specific moment without having acted prior to that is due to the limitation of our own intellect, and not, Heaven forbid, due to any deficiency in the Creator. This was the reasoning of **Aristotle**, who thought the world was eternal, believing that an agent must act continuously by nature. But this is not so, for the Creator created with a simple and free will, with hidden wisdom and desire. Within His power, He can change things, and it is no wonder that He acted at that specific time and not another. On this, our ancient sages, of blessed memory, said the world was created at the time <unclear>mentioned</unclear> <gloss>in the portion of Genesis</gloss>, etc., and there is no lack of Providence in this, etc., and he meant by this, etc.

---

<margin>And it is possible to say, etc.</margin>
In this, one must understand the distinction between that which acts by way of nature and that which acts by way of will. 
<margin>And it is a known thing, etc.</margin>
For anything that acts by nature must necessarily act continuously without interruption. 
And this is the "newness" etc. <insert>And it requires further study, etc.</insert> of the world, which was performed through absolute will. Do not wonder at this, for many things are beyond human intellect, etc. <margin>And see *The Guide for the Perplexed*, Part 2, etc. <note>The seminal philosophical work by Maimonides (Rambam)</note></margin> Regarding the matter of **Providence** <term>Providence: *Hashgacha*, God's supervision and intervention in the world</term>, he spoke briefly here, but perhaps he will expand further
