on the matter of angels and the act of Providence. One is not like the other, for this acts upon that, and this, etc. For the core principle is that all powers receive their strength from Him, blessed be He. Among these powers, there is a great distinction in how they influence the world. According to the opinion of our Sages, of blessed memory, there is both individual and general Providence <gloss>and it is well known</gloss> that all existing things are under His supervision. He gives life to them all and leads them according to His will. Do not say in your heart, Heaven forbid, that everything is mere chance, for everything is done through Providence from Him, blessed be He, <gloss>and specifically regarding man, etc.</gloss>
<margin>As it is written in Psalm 80, etc.</margin>
<insert>These matters are very deep, etc.</insert>
One who understands the secret of these matters will perceive and comprehend the difference between a natural action and a willful action. Furthermore, there is a distinction between a **Simple Agent** and a **Compound Agent**. A simple agent does everything according to its nature and there is no change in it, but a willful agent—possessing intellect and choice—acts at the time he desires. Even if he acts based on intellect, the intellect does not mandate the action at all times, but only at the time appropriate for it, etc. <margin>See **Radak** <note>Rabbi David Kimhi (1160–1235), a famous biblical commentator and grammarian</note>, etc.</margin> <insert>And great understanding is needed in this, etc.</insert>
From this we learn that although the action of the Creator, blessed be He, is of ultimate perfection and wisdom, this does not mandate that the world be eternal, Heaven forbid. Rather, it was created anew at the time His wisdom, blessed be He, desired.
<margin>**Rambam** wrote, etc. <note>Rabbi Moshe ben Maimon (Maimonides)</note></margin>
<insert>And this is the depth of the thought, etc.</insert>
<gloss>And as it is written in the *Wisdom of Solomon*, etc. <note>An apocryphal book often cited in medieval Jewish philosophy</note></gloss>

<summary>This page analyzes the theological conflict between the Aristotelian belief in an eternal universe and the Jewish belief in creation ex nihilo (from nothing). The author argues that while nature is constant, God operates through a "Free Will" that allows for a specific beginning to time and active Divine Providence.</summary>

<meta>The text on this page represents a shift from the philosophical inquiry on the previous page regarding the creation of the world to a technical legal analysis of Talmudic law. The author is now engaged in a "Pilpul" (analytical dialectic) concerning Tractate Shevuot, specifically focusing on the inclusion or exclusion of women in various types of oaths.</meta>

For if it were a common matter, there would be no need for a verse <note>a scriptural proof-text</note> regarding the **Oath of Testimony**, and the matter would not require etc. 

And so it is explained in the **Gemara** <note>the part of the Talmud that contains rabbinical analysis and commentary</note> on page 25: "If the verse is not relevant to the **Oath of Expression**, apply its meaning to the **Oath of Testimony**." This stands in opposition to the one who says, "We find in the entire Torah that Scripture equated woman to man." 

If that were so, why would a verse be necessary for the **Oath of Expression** <term>Oath of Expression: *Shevuat Bitui*, an oath regarding a person's own past or future actions, such as "I will eat" or "I did not eat"</term>? Perhaps it is because of the rule "We find etc."? Rather, it is certain that even though in the entire Torah, Scripture equated woman to man, regarding the **Oath of Expression**, we would not have known this. This is because the Scripture only made them equal regarding punishments <term>punishments: *onashin*, referring to legal penalties like lashes or execution</term>, as it is written etc. 

But here, regarding the obligation of a sacrifice <term>sacrifice: *korban*, an offering brought to the Temple to atone for an unintentional sin</term>, had the verse not said "Any man, any man" <note>original: *ish ish*</note>, we would not have known. Since the verse wrote "Any man, any man" regarding the **Oath of Expression**, we derive the inclusion of women from it, as is taught in the **Gemara** on the aforementioned page 25. And as for the **Oath of Testimony** <term>Oath of Testimony: *Shevuat HaEdut*, an oath taken by potential witnesses claiming they have no evidence to offer</term>, it is positioned near and linked by the Scripture to the **Oath of Expression**. Because of this, a woman is exempt from the **Oath of Testimony**, for it is written "Or a soul" <note>Hebrew: *o nefesh*</note> etc. 

See the **Tosafot** <note>medieval commentaries on the Talmud, primarily from the school of Rashi's descendants</note> on page 30a, in the entry beginning with "And they adjured them" etc., which states: "And if you should say, let the verse write 'woman' regarding the **Oath of Expression**, and let that case itself be derived 
<margin>
Who
shall sin
against a human
<note>Reference to Numbers 5:6</note>
</margin>
from the source where it is derived for the rest of the Torah" etc. See there. 

Also, the **Ramban** <note>Rabbi Moshe ben Nachman, 13th-century scholar</note> of blessed memory and the **Rashba** <note>Rabbi Shlomo ben Aderet, a leading 13th-century legal authority</note> of blessed memory raised this difficulty and struggled with it; see their words there. Also, in the **Tosafot HaRosh** <note>commentary by Rabbi Asher ben Yehiel</note> of blessed memory, he raised this difficulty; see his answer there. 

And it seems to me, in my humble opinion, that it can be said that if not for the verse "Any man, any man," I would have said that the **Oath of Expression** is not considered a matter of "punishment," because it does not carry the penalty of death or lashes. But perhaps, since there is a sacrifice involved, it might be considered a punishment. However, regarding the **Oath of Testimony**, which has no lashes etc., and since there is no punishment, we do not derive it from the general equality found in the rest of the Torah, where punishment is written. Because of this, I would have said that a woman is not obligated etc. 

This is simple and a clear matter. And as for what **Rashi** <note>the preeminent 11th-century commentator Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki</note> wrote on page 30 in the entry "In the Oath of Testimony," that a woman is not eligible to testify etc., see there and you will find that it is specifically in the **Oath of Testimony** that she is exempt because she is not an eligible witness. Because of this, we would not have known etc. But regarding the **Oath of Expression**, everyone knows that a woman is obligated etc. 

And as for what the **Gemara** says, "And perhaps we find that Scripture equated..." this is to say, perhaps that rule of the **Oath of Expression** comes from that general equality etc. 

Furthermore, it seems to me that regarding the **Oath of Expression**, the inclusion does not come because of punishment, but because it is written therein "Or a soul" etc., and we interpret it etc., in every place where it is written "Or a soul" etc., as will be explained in the **Mishnah** <note>the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions</note> and in the **Gemara** later on, with the help of Heaven. 

Furthermore, it appears that when it says, "And perhaps we find etc.," his intention is etc. For if it is derived from that verse, if so, what is the advantage etc.? For it can be said: admittedly, for the **Oath of Expression** the verse comes etc., but for the **Oath of Testimony**, where she is exempt etc. And there is more to be said etc.

<meta>The previous page ended with a series of Ladino (Judeo-Spanish) legal or administrative notes, likely a ledger or correspondence practice. For the 7th of <term>Cheshvan</term><note>The second month of the Hebrew calendar, occurring in mid-autumn.</note>. And what we wish to explain regarding the other days: It is proper in every single month to always reflect during the transitional days <note>original: "yomei de-iburei," referring to the days when one month transitions into the next.</note> as the month of God passes, since "what was, was," and what is to come is arriving. On the 2nd of Cheshvan, since a person has the leisure to contemplate the ways of the Name <note>A common Jewish substitution for the tetragrammaton (God's name).</note>, may He be blessed, concerning all that has passed over him during the days of his <term>winter</term><note>The Hebrew word "choref" can refer to the winter season or the "winter of one's life," meaning their youth or past history.</note> and all his conduct that has passed. He should strive in this from day to day until he becomes careful in all his conduct and all his affairs, and then he will be a complete man and a messenger.

And it is a "loss against its reward" <note>A reference to the Ethics of the Fathers (Pirkei Avot 2:1): "Calculate the loss of a commandment against its reward."</note> for if a man considers the loss of a <term>mitzvah</term><note>A religious commandment or good deed.</note> against its reward—for the reward is eternal and the loss is [only] worldly—the reward is everlasting. The reward for a mitzvah is nothing other than the mitzvah itself, and furthermore, one cannot estimate the magnitude of the reward for a mitzvah at all, for it is without limit. Man cannot grasp even a portion of it in this world, and although there is some slight understanding of it, nevertheless, it is impossible to truly grasp it. It is impossible to write even a hint of it, especially regarding the matter of serving the Name, may He be blessed, in truth and with a refined and pure thought according to one's strength and ability. For the essence of the service of God depends on this, as is known from many places, and particularly regarding these matters, see there. All this is to awaken hearts to a complete and true <term>repentance</term><note>original: "teshuvah"</note>, and not just to "sin with one's lips" <note>An idiom for a superficial confession where the mouth speaks but the heart does not follow.</note> like the way of other men, God forbid, who lie to their own souls. Rather, everything should be in truth with a whole heart, without any [ulterior] motive, but only for the honor of the Creator, may His name be blessed. This is the proper repentance, and may His name be blessed forever, Amen Selah, Hallelujah.

The same applies to all days, everything according to the above, and there is no need to elaborate on this. As can be seen in the prayer book of the <term>Ramak</term><note>Rabbi Moshe Cordovero (1522–1570), a preeminent kabbalist in Safed who wrote extensively on spiritual purification.</note>, of blessed memory, in the mystical intentions for the month of Elul. It is explicitly stated there, and whoever has merited to understand there will understand everything. The essence of everything is proper and pure repentance through Torah and commandments and in all his affairs for the sake of Heaven. So too during the days of the <term>Counting of the Omer</term><note>The 49-day period of spiritual preparation between the holidays of Passover and Shavuot.</note>, which are called "Days of Purity" as it is written in the Torah: "And it shall be on that day," and "Seven complete Sabbaths shall they be." Since a person purifies his soul from all dross and blemish, everything through his pure thought and his good deeds performed for their own sake. Amen, so may it be His will, forever and ever.

And this is the good path to serve one's Creator, may He be blessed and may His name be exalted above all blessing and praise. Through this, one will be desired and accepted before the Lord his Creator. May it be the will [of God], Amen Selah, Hallelujah. The Name, may He be blessed and may His name be blessed, "performs wonders alone, for His mercy endures forever," and "performs great things beyond searching out." May the Name be in our mouths always, so that we may know that all this is "vanity of vanities, all is vanity," <note>A famous quote from the biblical Book of Ecclesiastes (Kohelet) regarding the fleeting nature of physical existence.</note> except for proper repentance to serve Him, may His name be blessed and exalted. Amen and Amen.

<meta>While the previous page focused on spiritual reflections regarding the soul and the month of Cheshvan, this page shifts abruptly to a technical legal analysis of Talmudic law. It specifically examines the laws of testimony and the credibility of a father regarding the identity of his children as discussed in the tractates of Baba Batra and Ketubot.</meta>

<margin>
<unclear>For example</unclear>, the <unclear>laws</unclear> in [Tractate] Baba Batra, page 134
</margin>

Since we learn regarding witnesses from the Torah [that they testify] "from their writing" and not "from the [mouths of other] witnesses," etc. And even so, it teaches us that he is trusted <note>The Hebrew term is *ne'eman*, meaning legally credible or believed by the court.</note> even though there are witnesses who contradict him; he is trusted to say "this is my son," for he is believed regarding him as if he were two [witnesses], etc. And there is a difficulty from what Rav Yehuda said in the name of Shmuel in [Tractate] Baba Batra, page 134a, that he is trusted even though there are witnesses who contradict him. And this is what he says: since there is <insert>for this is what he says</insert> since it is explicitly stated in the Torah: "He shall acknowledge" (<term>Yakir</term><note>original: "Yakir" (Deuteronomy 21:17). This biblical law grants a father the unique legal power to identify his firstborn son for inheritance purposes, even if it contradicts social expectations.</note>), [meaning] he shall make him known to the heirs—that he is trusted—for this reason, he is trusted even against witnesses. But here in [Tractate] Ketubot, since there is no verse [stating] that he is trusted, for this reason it can be said that there is some logic regarding a father, and for that reason he is trusted.

***

These are the words of the Rashbam <note>Rabbi Shmuel ben Meir (c. 1085 – c. 1158), a major French commentator and grandson of Rashi.</note> there in Baba Batra, page 134b, in the heading beginning "And that which was taught: he is trusted," etc., who wrote: "and by the power of 'he shall acknowledge' (*yakir*), he is trusted," until here [is his quote]. And the Tosafot <note>Medieval analytical commentaries on the Talmud that often challenge or expand upon Rashi and Rashbam's explanations.</note> there raised a difficulty against him in the heading "And that which was taught," etc., for if so, why did Rav Yehuda say in the name of Shmuel: "for what legal purpose [was this said]," etc.? And it requires further study.

And that which we learned in the Mishnah: "If he said, 'this is my son,' he is trusted," etc., and Rav Yehuda said that the verse states "He shall acknowledge" (*yakir*)—he shall make him known to others, etc. And according to this logic of the Rashbam, we must say: [it is] specifically in Baba Batra, where there is a verse [stating] that he is trusted, that he is trusted even against witnesses. But here in [Tractate] Ketubot, where there is no verse, he is not trusted.

And then there is a further difficulty from that which was taught there on page 135: "He is trusted to say 'this is my son' and 'this is my brother'"; and there, the verse is only written regarding "my son," and it is specifically regarding "my son" that he should be trusted; so why was it taught that he is trusted regarding "my brother" as well? And it says there in the Gemara <note>The section of the Talmud that provides commentary and discussion on the Mishnah.</note> on page 135 that he is trusted to say "this is my brother," but he is only trusted [insofar as] to take his [own] portion with him <note>Meaning his testimony can allow a new person to share in his own inheritance portion, but it cannot decrease the portion of other existing heirs who do not consent.</note>. And it implies there that it is possible to say here in Ketubot also that we should say this: that he is trusted to say "this is my son." 

And that which we learned later on page 135a: that he is trusted to say "this is my brother," but he is only trusted to inherit with him and not to cause loss to others. And here too, let us say this: that he is trusted to say "this is my son" to exempt her [his wife] from <term>Chalitzah</term><note>A ritual ceremony that releases a childless widow from the obligation of marrying her deceased husband's brother.</note> and from <term>Yibum</term><note>Levirate marriage; the biblical requirement for a man to marry the childless widow of his deceased brother to continue the brother's line.</note>, but not to cause loss to others, such as to her relatives, etc. And with this, the difficulty against the Rashbam is well-resolved from what is [stated] there on page 135, where Rav Yosef said: "Even against witnesses, he is trusted to say 'this is my son' because 'He shall acknowledge' (*yakir*) is written concerning him." But here in [Tractate] Ketubot, where "He shall acknowledge" (*yakir*) is not written, he is not trusted against witnesses. And this is what is said here: that only when there are witnesses who do not know about him [the son] is he trusted; but in a place [where there are] witnesses [who contradict him], he is not trusted. And examine this well.

<summary>This page analyzes the legal credibility of a father's testimony regarding his children's identity, comparing the biblical principle of "Yakir" (acknowledgment) in Tractate Baba Batra with the laws of marriage and inheritance in Tractate Ketubot. The author reconciles conflicting views by the Rashbam and Tosafot, distinguishing between cases where testimony affects inheritance versus marital status or the rights of others.</summary>

The youth who wrote on the Sabbath day what was spoken, and he also acts here [in this capacity], for

that even though it was written on the Sabbath, the act stands <note>The Hebrew "ma'aseh kayam" refers to the principle that even if an act was performed through a transgression, its legal result may still be valid.</note>; nevertheless, in this place there are transgressors and those who "break the fence" <note>An idiom for those who violate communal norms or religious boundaries.</note>
who are wicked. It is proper to uphold the matter so that it will be known to all who come,

And behold, I have seen a legal ruling of Rabbi Isaac HaLevi <note>original: "Mahari HaLevi"—likely referring to a noted rabbinic authority, possibly the 11th-century teacher of Rashi or a later namesake.</note>, of blessed memory, who wrote thus in these words:
"A case occurred involving one who wrote a legal ruling on the Sabbath day and commanded the messenger to deliver

now from the fact that he gave the letter on the Sabbath, even though he gave it via a messenger; for the legal ruling

And if one did so, his punishment shall be thus: the legal ruling shall be null and void. And regarding this case,
when the judge saw it, he said: "Is this not a legal ruling written on the Sabbath?" and they disqualified it entirely.

And he further raised the matter of that which was written on the Sabbath day by means of a messenger:
Regarding the legal ruling in this matter, it appeared to my eyes that if the writing is valid—and it certainly is—

the judgment on the Sabbath day, nevertheless the ruling is true and one must fulfill the ruling. And even though
it was written on the Sabbath—for even though a transgression was certainly committed, the act stands—nevertheless
it is proper to say this: those who write on the Sabbath are all "debtors of the skull" <note>original: "chayyavei karkafta"—a Talmudic term for those who are severe sinners or who evade communal obligations.</note>.

It appeared that at the time they gave him the document, he said: "Let such-and-such a debt be paid."
[It was not] the Sabbath, but rather it was a legal ruling [from before], and he said: "What difference does it make to us? On the eve of the Sabbath he ruled."
The ruling—even though he gave the letter into the hand of the messenger on the Sabbath—since it was not *written* on the Sabbath,
it is permitted. And one should not be strict in this unless it is known to us that he wrote the ruling on the
Sabbath and they signed it. And when I was a youth <note>original: "kad havina talya"—an Aramaic expression for "when I was a young student."</note>, I saw this case in the study hall,

who permitted this, permitted it with common sense, because matters of a legal ruling are valid on the
Sabbath day, for even a legal ruling is nothing more than instruction and a mere saying. And since it is so,

<meta>The previous page discussed whether a legal verdict written on the Sabbath by a messenger remains valid or requires further investigation. In a session of three judges sitting together <note>original: "Be-moshav tlata kachada"—an Aramaic legal formula indicating a full quorum for a rabbinic court.</note>, we, the undersigned of the court <term>Beit Din<note>original: "Beit Din"—a Jewish rabbinic court of law.</note>, were present when the honorable Rabbi Baruch Caro came before us. He brought in his hand this written and signed deed, as follows:

How the aforementioned honorable Rabbi Baruch [purchased] from Habib Eliezer Habib, with witnesses signed upon it, stating:
"We, the undersigned witnesses, testify how the aforementioned Rabbi Habib, of his own free will and full desire, with a whole heart and without any coercion, sold, handed over, and finalized a complete and absolute sale for all eternity to the aforementioned honorable Rabbi Baruch Caro, regarding his portion in the place called 'The Apartment.' This is the apartment of the aforementioned Rabbi Hanina and all that belongs to it; they have acquired the apartment and all its attachments."
~~The aforementioned house and all its rights and everything that belongs to it from the house of the aforementioned Rabbi Hanina to the aforementioned Rabbi Baruch.~~
~~Which belongs to the aforementioned Rabbi Baruch as a complete and absolute gift, and they ratified it and established it in court as required.~~

And behold, witnesses came before us and confirmed their signatures in court, testifying how the aforementioned honorable Rabbi Baruch purchased from the aforementioned Rabbi Habib. This was by means of the sale that the aforementioned honorable Rabbi Baruch purchased from the hand of the aforementioned Habib, as written above by the witnesses—that the aforementioned Habib sold to the aforementioned Rabbi Baruch his portion of the apartment belonging to him and all that he has in the aforementioned alleyway <term>Mavoi<note>original: "Mavoi"—an alley or courtyard area shared by several residents, often subject to specific Jewish property laws.</note> called 'Rabbi Hanina.'

However, when the aforementioned deed was written, it was written in error. It has now become clear to us through the aforementioned witnesses that the error written in the deed is a <term>scribal error<note>original: "ta'ut sofer"—a technical legal term for a clerical mistake that does not affect the underlying validity of a transaction if the intent can be proven.</note>. It has now been established in court, as the witnesses came before us in the court and confirmed their signatures anew. Everything was established in the court with justice and integrity, written and signed in truth and fairness: that the entire aforementioned apartment belongs to the aforementioned honorable Rabbi Baruch, and his rights in it are great, specifically in the house of the aforementioned Rabbi Hanina, according to what is written in the deed. We have accepted their testimony according to the law and statutes.

Everything is valid, clear, and established <note>original: "sharir ve-barir ve-kayam"—a standard closing formula for Jewish legal documents to prevent tampering.</note>. ## 4. The Festivals

This relates to the Sukkah and to the 248 positive commandments, which are the aspect of Sovereignty in this world. This is the reason why we call the Sukkah the "Shadow of Faith" <note>original: "Tzilla de-Meheimanuta"—a Zoharic term for the Sukkah as a place of Divine protection and presence</note>, which is Sovereignty in the aspect of Kindness <term>Chesed<note>The attribute of expansive benevolence and love</note></term>.
Through this, one merits Understanding <term>Binah<note>The attribute of intellectual analysis and the source of the lower emotions</note></term>, which is the "Higher Kindness" and the "Fifty Gates of Understanding." This is the concept of the Sukkah: to be inside the Sukkah. This is the explanation of the Festivals, which contain the five levels explained above. They are fourteen, which are the aspect of the years of the aspect of Kindness and Might <note>original: "Chagat," usually an acronym for Chesed, Gevurah, and Tiferet—the emotional attributes</note>. There are three aspects to the Festivals, namely the three walls of the Sukkah, which represent the aspect of Understanding—that is, the Understanding required to become the aspect of the Sukkah as mentioned. And this is what was said in the name of my teacher, may his memory be a blessing, <unclear>according to the Sovereignty</unclear>.

It appears to be a legal or homiletic treatise, possibly a personal notebook or a response (responsa), focusing on civil law and the reconciliation of various authorities like Maimonides, Rashi, and the Zohar. The author, Moshe Suriano, concludes the section with a formal signature and date.</meta>

<margin>Yakir</margin> will enter into it from all that was said in this by **the Guide** <note>original: המורה (Ha-Moreh). This almost certainly refers to Maimonides (Rabbi Moses ben Maimon, 1138–1204) and his philosophical work, *The Guide for the Perplexed*.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, and these are the things which this Teacher composed, and this is their explanation with a perfect heart. For even though it is written that anyone who has a dispute with his fellow, and so-and-so denied this, he is not exempt until he returns the money; and this is what they said, and **the Rosh** <note>Rabbi Asher ben Jehiel (c. 1250–1327), a major halakhic authority.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, explained in this, may his memory be a blessing, they said something similar to this. It was not his intention to say that his fellow did it of his own will; and if so, for this was said in the name of those who said it, may their memory be a blessing <gloss>as mentioned above</gloss>. And behold, the words of the **Talmud** <note>original: ש"ס (Shas), an acronym for the "Six Orders" of the Mishnah, referring to the Talmud.</note> and their intention are well-clarified. And Kahana, may his memory be a blessing, said something similar to this <gloss>as mentioned above</gloss> <gloss>and it appears</gloss>. And behold, Rabbi Yehuda was striving to explain the words of Rabbi Yochanan. In his kindness, Rabbi Yehuda explained and holds that this is what is written: that anyone who holds the money of his fellow in his hand... and afterwards I saw in the words of the explanation that he elevated his words <gloss>according to</gloss> the truth of the plain meaning; and so testified **the R"I** <note>Rabbi Isaac of Dampierre, a famous Tosafist.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, and Hezekiah.

<margin>And more</margin> during the lifetime of Rabbi Yehuda his master, and so it is written in the aforementioned chapter, and there is no refuting him in this matter at all, <margin>as is written</margin> concerning them. **Our Master Rashbi** <note>Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai, the 2nd-century sage to whom the Zohar is traditionally attributed.</note> said <gloss>according to</gloss> the truth, and behold, it came as a hint to him that what **Rashi** <note>Rabbi Shlomo Yitzhaki (1040–1105), the preeminent commentator on the Bible and Talmud.</note>, may his memory be a blessing, wrote above in Chapter 8 of *Those*, that the matter should be <gloss>in this</gloss> which is written "in his hand." Behold, their opinion is well-clarified in what Rashi, may his memory be a blessing, wrote, and what is written in Chapter 8 <gloss>in this</gloss>, for since it went out, the judgment went out upon him, meaning <gloss>in what</gloss> we wrote above.

<summary>This page contains a Rabbinic legal and mystical discourse by Moshe Suriano, reconciling the opinions of authorities like Maimonides, Rashi, and the Zohar on matters of civil liability and debt. It concludes with a formal date and signature in 1796 CE, marking a transition from the Christian texts found earlier in the manuscript.</summary>

he wrote the following: 'And see what **Nachmanides** <note>Rabbi Moses ben Nachman (1194–1270), a leading medieval scholar</note>, of blessed memory, wrote in his novellae <note>Original: "Chidushim," legal insights or creative interpretations</note> on the Tractate **Nedarim** <note>The volume of the Talmud dealing with Vows</note>,

For behold, **Maimonides**, of blessed memory, wrote in Chapter 2 of the Laws of Vows as follows: 'And even though
he vowed an animal as a prohibition, and it was as a **Nazirite** <note>One who takes a vow of abstinence</note> or as a sacrifice <note>original: "Korban"</note>...' and these are the words of **Maimonides**, of blessed memory:
'Therefore, anyone who associates <term>Matpis (associates/attaches)</term> an object with something already forbidden by a vow, it is as if he is making a vow himself, etc.'

<summary>This page consists of dense Rabbinic legal annotations cross-referencing various authorities on the mechanics of making vows (Nedarim) and the substitution of sacrificial animals (Temurah). It specifically focuses on the concept of "associating" a new object with an existing vow and explores contradictions between Maimonides and later commentators like the Magen Avraham.</summary>

Speech, and this shall be very good for all the good people. When one does not go the right way, one should not fear, and then the Torah is indeed the best thing that a person can learn in the world, and learn all things in it that are written here, [in order] to lead a good life. Afterwards comes the discourse <unclear>X</unclear> regarding the person, when the person will go before God, may He be blessed, and will <unclear>X</unclear> before our God, may He be blessed. There the person will see that his life was very short, and that all the things he had in this world are absolutely nothing. For when the person departs from this world, nothing remains of him except for all the good that he had [done] in his life. Afterwards he will see his merit before God, may He be blessed, and he will be given his reward <term>sechar: spiritual reward in the afterlife</term> for [being among] the good people. Amen, Selah <note>Selah: a Hebrew liturgical term often used to conclude a prayer or emphasize a point</note>.

<meta>The previous page transitioned from technical legal discussions on vows into a more accessible, moralizing tone. This page continues that shift, moving fully into a Hebrew ethical treatise (Musar) that emphasizes the spiritual protection offered by Torah study. It draws on the authority of the "Beit Yosef" (Rabbi Joseph Karo) and the Zohar to argue that the Torah is the only reliable defense against the human impulse to do wrong.</meta>

What our master <term>Maran: a title of high honor for a great sage, here referring to Rabbi Joseph Karo</term> wrote in the book *Beit Yosef* <term>Beit Yosef: "House of Joseph," a foundational 16th-century commentary on Jewish law</term>, section 244: there he wrote that the Torah is the path by which we entered this world, and we must not walk any other path but its own.

And this is the straight and correct path upon which every person can stand, and in it one will find rest for his soul, by virtue of what he possesses of the correct Torah. It cleanses his heart so that the Evil Inclination <term>Yetzer HaRa: the innate human impulse toward selfishness or sin</term> has no permission to wage any war against him.

<unclear>War</unclear> and joy; and this is what the scripture means: "The testimony of the Lord is sure, making wise the simple" <note>original: Psalm 19:7</note>. Through the foolishness within them, people come to sin; but once one learns Torah, he is already far removed from transgression. With correct understanding, even when he is alone in his house, in the innermost chambers <note>original: "hadrei hadarim," implying complete privacy</note>, even though no one sees him, he commits no sin. For a person cannot simply do as he pleases through the aforementioned path, except when he sees that this path is the correct one in which he will find rest for his soul.

And when he looks into all the words of our Sages and the stories of their deeds, he will see and understand what those actions bring to a person, and he will grasp this well. Also in the *Zohar* <term>Zohar: the foundational work of Jewish mysticism, or Kabbalah</term> in the portion of Noah <note>referring to the weekly Torah portion "Noach"</note>, it is stated that a person cannot refrain from transgression unless he occupies himself with Torah, because the Torah is what nullifies the Evil Inclination from the heart of man. For as long as a person is occupied with it, the Evil Inclination cannot overpower him. And so it is.

---

And therefore it was written in the aforementioned book *Beit Yosef* that this is the straight path, etc. There is a further detail to consider: when one looks at the deeds of our aforementioned Sages, it is written there that they learned this path from the "holy ones on high," our holy teachers. For them, the Holy Torah was a refuge and a hiding place from all sin.

And this is what King David, peace be upon him <note>original: "a"h" (alav ha-shalom)</note>, said in Psalms, Chapter 128: "Blessed is every one that feareth the Lord; that walketh in His ways." The meaning is that great fear [of God] obligates one to walk in His ways, lest he stumble, God forbid <note>original: "h"v" (chas ve-chalila)</note>, amidst the multitude of actions to which he has accustomed himself. And thus it is said that the Torah is the shield against all kinds of calamities in the world, and through it we see that a person will truly merit to be one who walks in the ways of the Lord with integrity.

<meta>The previous page discussed Kabbalistic theories regarding the soul (Nefesh, Ruach, and Neshama) and how physical actions connect to the Sefirot (divine emanations). This page shifts from theoretical mysticism to practical communal application, detailing the establishment of a "Holy Society" (Chevra Kadisha) in the year 1670 to provide spiritual and physical support for its members during times of illness and distress.</meta>

->With the Help of the Blessed Name<-

For the sake of a holy servant, a custom was established to found a single society on the sixteenth day of the month of Kislev <note>November/December</note> in the year 5430 <note>1669/1670 CE</note>. From now and forever, it shall be said of them and their children after them that they shall stand as a memorial before the Lord always, an eternal act of righteousness for those who love His name.

In the year 5430, a law and a statute were written by the men of the holy community <term>Kehal: the organized Jewish communal structure</term> as a memorial for eternal generations. It shall be within the power of every single member of the aforementioned Holy Society <term>Chevra Kadisha: literally "Holy Society," here referring to a mutual aid and spiritual fellowship</term> that if, Heaven forbid, any person among the members of the aforementioned Holy Society should be struck by any illness or trouble to scour away his sins in the "Chamber of the Name" <note>An idiom for divine judgment or the spiritual realm</note>—for though judgment may be decreed upon his physical body from Heaven—he nevertheless has companions who will "gird their loins" for him. This is not according to our deserts, but rather like the prayer of Moses our Teacher <note>original: "Mo-she Rabbenu"</note>. Through their merit, perhaps God will be comforted and save them from their troubles, as it is said in His Holy Torah: "And the Lord said to Moses: Make for yourself a fiery serpent... and it shall be that everyone who is bitten, when he looks upon it, shall live" <note>Numbers 21:8</note>. For it is known that in this land, there is no power unless it comes from the Exalted side, which is built above intellect and wisdom. And so, if anyone lifts his face toward Heaven and prays, he shall escape from distress.

Therefore, all the leaders of the community gathered together and built a city and a fortress—the "pillars of the world"—so that they might bring salvation in times of distress and peace to our children. May the Lord be around them to protect against every evil occurrence, just as our holy forefathers did; they would carry and look upon the banner in war and be victorious. So too do we walk in the name of the Lord our God as a memorial for our generations. They showed our generations the construction of our houses, just as in the work of our own generation; we have arrived and the building still stands. Praise His name, for He has shown strength and glory to manifest to every generation that the hand of the Lord is strong with him, granting him rest from all his enemies roundabout and giving him an inheritance.

Give honor to the generation that goes and the one that comes. Any man of the "Chosen People" <note>original: "Anshei Segulah"</note> who desires to immerse his soul in the perfect Torah of the Lord, which sustains the soul in life and through good deeds—such as changing the name <term>Shinuy HaShem: a ritual of renaming a sick person to confuse the Angel of Death</term> and changing the location <term>Shinuy Makom: moving a sick person to a new home for spiritual protection</term>, according to the practices of our Sages of blessed memory. They "gave an eye for an eye" <note>Used here metaphorically for intense focus or empathy</note> and suffered afflictions with love. Charity <term>Tzedakah</term> saves from death, and the merit of those who speak, vow, and pray with the sick person shall strengthen and heal the sick of His people Israel.

<margin>And in order</margin> that they should not be ashamed to increase their prayer and their general charity—for whoever has the means to give, let him give willingly and of his own soul, without any compulsion or recognition. Let the holy community, each according to his strength, contribute whether through charity, support, money, or physical service. And he who does not have the means, let him increase his prayer before the Merciful and Gracious God. It is fitting for every single city to join itself to the aforementioned community. He has the power in these holy days to be present in times of joy and also in times of trouble, Heaven forbid.

Therefore, we have gathered into one society and returned to the good path of the congregation. Our mouths are filled with will, and a generous spirit shall grace us <unclear>with His goodness</unclear>. Therefore, we have come to sign in this land on the aforementioned day in the month of Adar <note>February/March</note>, 5430. May the pleasantness of the Lord be upon us; establish the work of our hands for us, indeed, establish the work of our hands. This is the <unclear>word</unclear> of our community, which we have written and signed above in the aforementioned year 5430. May his name be remembered for generation after generation <unclear>forever</unclear> among our brothers, the Children of Israel. In times of joy and times of favor, may You wipe away our enemies from before our eyes, and <unclear>grant merit</unclear> to our children through vision and acts of loving-kindness. We shall uphold this in faith with the help of the Blessed Name, on Sunday, the sixteenth of the month of Elul <note>August/September</note>, in the year 5430.

<summary>This page records the formal founding and regulations of a Holy Society (Chevra Kadisha) in 1670, established to provide mutual aid, prayer, and ritual support for sick members. It emphasizes the power of communal unity, charity, and specific rituals like name-changing to avert divine decrees and ensure the community's survival.</summary>

<keywords>Chevra Kadisha, communal regulations, 1670, charity, prayer for the sick, Shinuy HaShem, Kislev, Adar, Elul, Jewish mutual aid societies</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the founding of a "Holy Society" (Chevra Kadisha) in 1670, emphasizing mutual aid and prayer during times of illness, drawing on the merit of Moses. This page shifts from communal bylaws to a homiletic discourse (Derush) on the metaphysical primacy of the Torah as the blueprint for creation.</meta>

->**A Discourse on the Excellence of the Torah**<-

A discourse on the excellence of the Torah, for the Holy One, Blessed be He, created the world by means of the Torah. This is the secret of the five days of labor <note>original: "chameshet yimei ha-ma'aseh"; typically the "six days of creation," this may refer to a specific mystical grouping or a scribal shorthand for the work week</note> and this is what you see regarding the forty days at the standing at Mount Sinai, etc. "And Moses was on the mountain forty days" <note>original: "vayehi Moshe ba-har arbaim yom"; Exodus 24:18</note>. And behold, Moses corresponds to every person, even though he was poorer and humbler than all men. This is the secret of the forty days; as it is written: "And Moses was on the mountain forty days and forty nights." In all of this Torah, everything is hinted at, and all of this hints at Him, Blessed be He—meaning, that by means of the Torah he was strengthened within it. Such was the intention of what He gave; and he would say: "What use to me is the vanity and falsehood of this world and all its business? I shall occupy myself with the Torah." 

This is what is found in tradition: that every man of Israel, according to his own value, should contemplate the Lord with his intellect and see His works. This is the opening of the gate to the light of the Torah, and he shall understand it by saying: "Behold the excellence of the Torah and how it is greater than everything." Behold, in the statement of our Teacher, of blessed memory <term>Rabbenu: likely referring to Maimonides or a central communal sage</term>, one who strives in the Torah is essentially striving in life itself, and this is the blessing. Likewise, "And he shall live by them" <note>original: "v'chai ba-hem"; Leviticus 18:5</note> refers to his Torah, for the commandment is the lamp and the Torah is the great light. 

Regarding this it was said that for the excellence of the Torah, everything was created. It was the blueprint <note>original: "mivneh"; literally the structure or building plan</note> for the whole world, and through it, Man was created. Behold, all excellences are within it, as it is written: "Behold the way to our Holy Torah," and behold, one must honor it, for everything depends upon it. This is the meaning of the verse: "The heavens for height, and the earth for depth" <note>Proverbs 25:3</note>, etc., for all of these came for the service of the Creator. 

Thus, we have spoken of the excellence of the Torah, because for its sake the entire world was created, in short: all excellence belongs to the Torah alone, for everything that exists in the world is for the sake of the Torah. This is the meaning of "In the beginning God created" <note>Genesis 1:1</note>, and likewise the Jerusalem Translation <note>original: "Targum Yerushalmi"; an ancient Aramaic translation of the Pentateuch</note> renders it: "With wisdom God created everything" <note>original: "be-chokhmata"; translating the Hebrew "Bereshit" not as 'In the beginning' but 'With Wisdom,' which is identified with the Torah</note>. This is the wisdom of the Torah. Everything comes from the Holy One, Blessed be He, so that man may be able to serve and fulfill His commandments. 

In this way, a man can achieve great excellence. Included in this is that everything written in the Holy Torah is entirely truth and justice, and one must believe in everything that appears within it. Our Sages of blessed memory said regarding the verse: "The fear of the Lord is pure, enduring forever" <note>Psalm 19:10</note>, that the Torah is eternal. Likewise, King David, peace be upon him, warns regarding this path in Psalm 119: "Happy are those whose way is upright" <note>original: "ashrei temimei derech"</note>, etc. All these matters pertain to the excellence of the Torah and the commandment. Therefore, King David said in that Psalm that there you will find everything. He said: "For the commandment is a lamp and the Torah is light" <note>Proverbs 6:23</note>, and it is the great light that will shine for human beings in this world and in the World to Come. In this way shall a man walk, and it will be good for him in his world, and the verse shall be fulfilled in him: "For they are life to those who find them" <note>Proverbs 4:22</note>, etc. 

The explanation of this statement is simple: it is what the Sage wrote in the Book of Proverbs: "The Lord by wisdom founded the earth" <note>original: "Hashem be-chokhma yasad aretz"; Proverbs 3:19</note>, etc. In this way, he shall have life and peace due to the excellence of the Torah. This is the foundation of everything, and regarding this it was said: for the excellence of the Torah, the world was created, etc.

<summary>This page presents a homily on the "Excellence of the Torah," arguing that the Torah served as the divine blueprint for Creation and remains the source of all life and spiritual light. It emphasizes that both the physical world and the life of Moses serve as "hints" toward the Torah's eternal importance.</summary>

a Sukkah, merits to be in the aspect of the "Shadow of the Holy One, Blessed be He," etc. Look closely there in *Likutey Moharan*, Lesson 1, 
that all of a person’s actions in this world should be entirely **<insert>in awe</insert>** etc., the secret of the Holy Blessed One,

### As written by us in Chapter <unclear>28</unclear> <unclear>there</unclear>

As is written by us in this composition that follows hereafter, these are the words of the ruling of our teacher and master, Rabbi **<term>Joseph Karo<note>1488–1575; the author of the Shulchan Aruch, the foundational code of Jewish law.</note></term>**, of blessed memory:

Regarding the law of one who **betroths** a woman with a **document** <note>Hebrew: *shtar*. In Jewish law, marriage can be effected through money, a document, or physical intimacy.</note> that was written **without her knowledge** or consent <unclear>etc.</unclear>
As it is found in the **<term>Tur<note>The *Arba'ah Turim*, a major 14th-century code of Jewish law by Jacob ben Asher.</note></term>** (*Even HaEzer*, Chapter 28, Section 3), these are his words: One who betroths a woman with a document—so wrote the **<term>Rambam<note>Rabbi Moshe ben Maimon (Maimonides, 1138–1204), one of the most influential Torah scholars of the Middle Ages.</note></term>**, of blessed memory, in a response: that one who betroths a woman with a document and sanctifies her with it, she is certainly **fully betrothed** <note>Hebrew: *mekudeshet*. A state where she is legally bound to the man and requires a divorce to marry anyone else.</note> and does not need to repeat the act and betroth her with money.

But in this case before us, it appears that there is **no betrothal** <note>Hebrew: *kiddushin*.</note> here at all, because the document was not written with her knowledge, as stated in the *Tur* there <unclear>in Section 3</unclear>. And even though she said "yes" after she received it, it is of no avail, as the **<term>Rashba<note>Rabbi Solomon ben Adoret (1235–1310), a leading Spanish medieval scholar.</note></term>** wrote in a response brought by the **<term>Beis Yosef<note>The "House of Joseph," a comprehensive commentary on the Tur written by Joseph Karo.</note></term>** there. This is because it is like a man saying to a woman, "Be betrothed to me with money," and then giving her a security deposit instead—the betrothal does not take effect. 

I have seen in the books of the Sages that one should be strict because of a "doubtful betrothal," and she would therefore require a **<term>get<note>A formal Jewish bill of divorce.</note></term>**. Nevertheless, if he desired to arrange a match with her through the writing of the document written here, and the Great Teacher wrote to him regarding a case such as this one before us: it is of no use at all. 

The correct view is to say that she does not require a **get**, except for "clarity of the matter" <note>Hebrew: *le-revacha de-milta*. A legal precaution to remove any possible doubt, even if the law strictly says it is unnecessary.</note>. One must <strike><unclear>protest it</unclear></strike> [clarify] the matter: that according to the law, she does not require a **get** from him at all, but does so only to remove herself from concern and suspicion. If she does not, Heaven forbid she should act in such a way <note>i.e., marry someone else without this precautionary divorce</note>.

And so wrote the Great Teacher, the **<term>Mahar"a<note>Likely a reference to a prominent Ashkenazi authority, possibly Rabbi Isaac Aboab or another contemporary "Great Teacher."</note></term>**, of blessed memory, in a handwritten response, and these are his words: One who betroths a woman with money and she [is/says] <unclear>in the language of</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> and in these matters one should not say there is a doubt. Rabbi <unclear>Judah</unclear> says in this language: The law follows the words of the Sages who said that one must be strict in this. 

Even though the document was in his hand and he gave it to her with the language, "Behold, you are **betrothed** to me with this document," and even though the entire order of the betrothal is written within it, she is **not betrothed** unless the document was [written] with her knowledge and written specifically for her name for the sake of betrothal. If not, it is of no use at all. And if he went back and betrothed her with this same document another time after it was already in her hand, she is **not betrothed** at all, even though he betrothed her with it anew—since the document was originally written without her knowledge. But if she wishes for him to send her a **different document** that was made according to the law of betrothal, she is then certainly **betrothed**.

<summary>This page analyzes a legal dispute regarding whether a marriage is valid if the marriage contract (shtar) was written without the woman's prior consent. The text cites major authorities like Maimonides and Joseph Karo to conclude that such a betrothal is generally void, though a divorce document (get) might be given as a precaution to avoid scandal.</summary>

# Regarding Passover and the Commandment to Destroy Leaven

On Passover, in such a case, it is implied from the Torah—from the day that is designated for every person on Passover, and so on. Regarding a person, whenever he is found in these years, they must destroy <note>original: *leva’er*; referring to the removal or destruction of leaven</note> in advance and remove the **chametz** <term>leavened grain products forbidden on Passover</term>, etc. Those who fulfill the commandment properly, it is best that he burns [it], and all [prohibitions of] **"it shall not be seen"** <note>original: *D.B.Y.*, abbreviation for *De-Bal Yera'eh*, the prohibition against owning visible leaven</note> apply as well.

<margin>At the time</margin> He began with the commandment of destroying leaven according to the Torah <note>original: *de-oraita*; laws originating directly from the Pentateuch</note> in the **Gemara** <note>The component of the Talmud containing rabbinic analysis</note> of the first chapter of tractate **Pesachim** and at the mentioned time, etc. It can be said that according to the custom of places where they burn the leaven—every small or large thing—it would be the law to destroy everything. According to this custom, it relates to the prohibition of "it shall not be seen," for the commandment is fulfilled through the physical act. From this moment, his intention is to destroy it, and with this, everything is settled.

<margin>And all this</margin> **Rashi** <note>original: *P.H.*, likely referring to *Perush Ha-Kontres*, the standard title for Rashi's commentary</note> explained this: that if one destroys the leaven remaining for him which he does not desire, it is for the honor of the Festival. However, according to this custom, his will is expressed in his action, and from this moment his intention is to destroy [it]. Thus, there is no issue of **"it shall not be seen"** or **"it shall not be found"** <term>Bal Yera'eh and Bal Yimatze; the two biblical prohibitions against possessing leaven during Passover</term>, for he declares it ownerless <note>original: *mefakir*; the legal process of renouncing ownership</note> and has no desire for it at all. Even if he intended to destroy everything—although it is not strictly a commandment—nevertheless, the commandment is fulfilled in his action, and it is considered as if he destroyed every bit of leaven.

In this way, the words of the **Midrash** <note>Ancient rabbinic homiletical interpretations</note> are settled, which said: "And on the night of the 14th..." etc. According to this, he should not have had to destroy everything at one time, except to ensure he does not encounter a prohibition. This requires further study <note>original: *Tzarich Iyun*; a scholarly term for a point that remains unresolved</note> in Rashi's explanation. However, if not for this report, it would be the law to destroy it in actual practice, etc., for wherever the Scripture says "to destroy," it refers to the physical action, etc. By this, his intention is to destroy, and with this, everything is settled.

---

<margin>And it requires study</margin> Our Rabbis say that it is enough for him to burn the remainder, even though it is not strictly a commandment, nevertheless, etc. And how is it that even according to the one who says "the leaven of an Israelite" in the Gemara of the first chapter of Pesachim, and at the time he burns it, he intends to destroy it and has no desire for it at all? This requires further study. Based on this, what **Rashi** wrote in the first chapter of Pesachim is settled: that they are accustomed to destroy everything, etc.

How then is it necessary to destroy the rest, etc.? Here too, it is no better to destroy it in practice, etc. It can be said that the explanation of this report returns to all that the **Sages** <note>original: *Chazal*; the Jewish sages of antiquity</note> said: that regarding the commandment to destroy, the commandment is fulfilled most excellently in this way. **Rashi**, of blessed memory, in the first chapter of Pesachim, wrote that it is a commandment to destroy and there is no issue of "it shall not be seen" or "it shall not be found," etc. On this, the **Remez** <note>Rabbi Moshe Zacuto (c. 1625–1697), a prominent kabbalist and commentator</note> of blessed memory explained and said that even though there is no violation of "it shall not be seen," etc.

<summary>This page analyzes the legal mechanisms of Bi'ur Chametz (destroying leaven), debating whether the commandment is fulfilled through physical burning or mental renunciation, citing Rashi and the Remez.</summary>

And because of the syllable <term>Hi</term> <note>A Hebrew sound often associated with divine presence</note> that you called, O Lord, and may You give to his children’s children’s children. In <term>gematria</term> <note>The mystical Jewish system of assigning numerical values to letters</note>, the word "Yak" is equal to "Portion"; may peace appear before grace. And to say: to have mercy upon you, "Yak" is Grace, etc. Now we shall explain this joy of being secure in our complete happiness, as it were, in the name <term>Sulam</term> <note>meaning "Ladder"</note>. Its beauty in gematria is equal to the Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Name of God, Y-H-W-H</note>. Grace to his children in his righteousness. Behold, in our exile, grace upon grace is a unique thing from Him; strength and joy. And may he rejoice in the Lord, the God of Israel, the One who blesses and is blessed.

->The Youth said<- <note>original: *Ha-Mavach*, likely an acronym for a title or the author’s name</note>

He wrote: "Behold now, a living hand in the name of peace." Explain: my living hands are a hand, and blessed is their union. According to the words of joy, the <term>Peshat</term> <note>The literal or plain meaning of a text</note> is simple for a certain woman, as it were, in the abundance of His glory, in joy. Here are two joys, lest the aspect of grace—"He has been gracious to me"—is in the two letters <term>Yod-He</term>. Be strong! Grace is in Phinehas, and he rejoiced in his children. He is beloved to me in his coming... and may the God of the world live, and may he be a pious disciple <note>original: *hasid hanich*</note> from now on.

# Spiritual Unity

In spiritual wholeness <note>original: *b-kolnayta ukhnanit*</note> concerning Him for us in all things that come from God, which is His,

<meta>This page transitions back to Hebrew from the previous page's Syriac mystical text. ->Explanation of the Honorable Rabbi Isaac Hayyim Lampronti<-

On Sunday, the 28th day of the month of Sivan, in the year 5436 from the creation [1676 CE], here in the Holy Community of Ferrara <note>Ferrara, Italy, was a major center of Jewish life and scholarship during the Renaissance and early modern period</note>.
And this was copied from the manuscript written by the eminent scholar, the late Rabbi Zalman Cohen, who speaks of this writing found in the library, as follows: --- "And now, on Wednesday, the 3rd day of the month of Tammuz, I have given these few words to be examined by my friend, the eminent scholar, the Master Isaac Hayyim Lampronti <note>Isaac Hayyim Lampronti (1679–1756) was a world-renowned Italian rabbi and physician, best known for his massive alphabetical encyclopedia of Jewish law, *Pahad Yitzhak*</note>, may his light shine, who is praised in Torah and wisdom; may he live forever and merit the crown of the Torah."
"How glorious and how pleasant it is to see and look upon it, for his composition, his wisdom, and his secret—may He be blessed—testify for him. It is true, since for many years we have not heard of anyone who could write such a thing; no doubt this was from the Lord. I know that he is one of the members of the Academy <note>original: *Bnei HaYeshiva*</note> who stands before Him—may He be blessed—with a living spirit. I found this composition written in his hand, in the Aramaic language, and in a pure and sweet tongue for the reader, understandable to every examiner. Even if the examiner is brief, he will still comprehend and understand the heights of its subject matter."
"The meditation of his heart was a reflection of holy matters; it shall not depart from him except through understanding, like a strengthening fountain <note>a traditional Jewish metaphor for a scholar whose wisdom flows and increases continuously</note>, for it is in his hand to write and speak of profound matters, explaining clearly the literal and the allegorical meanings, for this composition follows the paths of Kabbalah <note>Jewish mysticism</note>, and they are wonderful things that restore the soul." --- "For this purpose, I have signed my name for glory and honor, in the month of Tammuz in the year 5436 [1676 CE]."
And I found another manuscript of the aforementioned writing, and it was written upon it as follows: "I have seen and looked upon this writing which was made by a most wise scholar; I do not know his name, but his excellence is recognizable through his words, which speak of the height of the subject of this document." And this is what was written in it: ---
"On Wednesday, the 13th day of the month of Tammuz, 5496 [1736 CE], my heart moved me to respond and to advocate with a pleasant eloquence, with a tongue of truth and justice, regarding this writing which was written by the scribe mentioned in the name of the aforementioned book, and I said: It is true."

---

"Public Domain," Chapter 7, 4 cubits <note>a measurement of about 6 feet; this is a reference to the laws of the Sabbath regarding carrying in public spaces</note> for the purpose of checking, which the Great Rabbi composed with a wide hand, as follows:
The known version in the "Marriage Contract of Kings," the matter of the building of the priesthood from heaven <note>original: *min shamaya*</note> before man; and this was his own handwriting... so that "no outcast be cast out from him" <note>a biblical quote from 2 Samuel 14:14, often used in mystical contexts to refer to the redemption of souls</note>; blessed is he!
Regarding this it is said: "Who among you is the Lord in his spirit, and the word of the Lord is in your mouths." Blessed are you, Israel, our brothers. The reward of this wise scribe is with the Lord, and he has a good and pleasant portion.
Specifically, regarding the precise detail with which he copied and added wisdom in his thought, and his secret is pure. He applied it to his thought and his mouth and wrote this document, which was his thought.
And I further saw that at the conclusion of this, there was found one book containing deep wisdom. Perhaps this scholar who composed the document is the one who copied the proofs in my eyes... and it was written at the end of the book: "Completed and finished, praise be to God, Creator of the world, Amen."
"I am giving this document as a gift to the Teacher and Master Solomon, son of the late Abraham Jonah, to keep the word of the Lord for me. And this is the marriage contract <note>original: *Ketubah* - likely used here as a metaphor for a binding spiritual or legal agreement</note> in truth, precision, and justice. Write within this book the aforementioned document and the ruling of the wonderful Rabbi."

# Part Two

To complete that which preceded: so that we might show that which we spoke of, a thing known to us all. In all the discourses <note>original: "memre," referring to formal theological speeches or homilies</note>—not only those regarding Ephesus <note>referring to the Council of Ephesus, 431 AD, which addressed the nature of Christ</note>—but in a way that shows the word <note>original: "ptagma," a specific phrase or decree</note> which is fitting for this section <note>original: "pasuqa," a division of text</note>, that we might see the blessedness of the Word <note>original: "Milta," the Syriac term for the Greek "Logos," referring to Christ</note>; that which is full, then, to show and to hear clearly. Along with the discourses, so that also the distinction <note>original: "parushuta," the act of discerning or distinguishing</note> of the passion <note>original: "hasha," referring to the suffering or the Crucifixion</note> of the Word itself might be seen as a division <margin>169</margin> <note>The marginal note "ܐܣܛ" (EST) likely serves as a reference number or line marker (stikhos)</note> to all of us. Those who gave rest to the Word—and the Word of that [one] is divine—so that they might hear with that which is possible, so that the appearance of it might be manifest to every person who was called to this matter. Even then, in the decision of time, the changing of the Word which is full from its own [nature], so as to show to us all that to it belongs all this—this discourse. Where everyone who is also in this discourse—this which precedes the truth which they heard—those who are full from its own, so that they might hear it: the Word that even now rules over that matter. So that they might hear it: the Word which is full from its own, so that also after that Word which belongs to this discourse of the division, not then to show its own, so as to see also in this discourse, this which is also in it, in the Word; also how to show it, the Word, also in this place, that they should be heard...

---

It is called, then, unto two: that which is also in the distinction of the passion of the Word, that which is—not then of the discourses of the two—of the discourse which preceded. That we might see, then, from the Word itself how it is in every place, and how in every place, also in this discourse, this [is] to show and to hear. In this discourse of the passion, concerning whom the Word is to show, and how it shall be heard in it, in the Word which preceded: that which is also from that matter, so as to hear in it, in the Word. Also from after that which preceded, so as to hear also in it, in the Word; so that also in this discourse of the passion we might see it, the Word. So that also that which preceded the discourses, how to see, then, in it, in the Word, that also in this discourse, this [is] not only to show that it is, but that also in the place—that which is in every place—from the Lord, the Word. In the whole discourse, to see in every place that it is, also in this discourse, this [is] to hear that also in this discourse, this Word is full, how...

May his name forever be remembered by all people, for that which is good and pleasing before the Blessed Name <note>original: "Ha-Shem Yitbarakh" - a common pious way to refer to God</note> is more than this.

There is another hint in what <term>King David, peace be upon him</term> <note>original: "DaMeA"H" – an acronym for David HaMelekh Alav HaShalom</note> wrote: "I have set the Lord always before me" (*Shiviti Hashem l'negdi tamid*).
It is known to all what the <term>Baal Shem Tov</term> <note>Rabbi Israel ben Eliezer (c. 1700–1760), the founder of the Hasidic movement</note> taught regarding the word *Shiviti* ["I have set"]: it is related to the word *Hishtavut* [equanimity]. This means that whether people praise him or despise him, it should all be the same to him. This is true equanimity.
And it is known that this equanimity is the essence and the inner core of divine service. Through this, one can reach the level of total self-nullification <note>original: "Bittul" – the mystical state of ego-transcendence or "nullifying" the self before the Divine</note> for the sake of His Blessed Name, with great fear and love.

---

And behold, there is a hint in the word *Shiviti* as a <term>Notarikon</term> <note>A system of shorthand where each letter of a word serves as an acronym for a full concept</note>: 
**Sh** is for *Shakhar* [Reward]; 
**V** is for *Ve-Onesh* [and Punishment]; 
**Y** is for *Yirah* [Fear/Awe]; 
**T** is for *Tamid* [Always]; 
**Y** is for *Yitbarakh* [The Blessed One]. 

This means that the matter of reward, punishment, and the fear of the Blessed Name should always be before him. Specifically, this refers to the highest level of fear called "the Fear of Shame"—a sense of bashfulness and awe before the Blessed Name, similar to what our Sages of blessed memory said: "Know before whom you stand." This is achieved through the practice of fear, and through this, one can reach the aforementioned sublime level of total self-nullification. This is the general rule of constant service of God.

The principle of the specific and the general <note>original: "Prat u-Klal" - a classic Rabbinic method of interpreting legal texts</note> from world to eternity. 
Concerning this, the meticulous scholars <note>those who are exacting in their study of grammar or law</note> arrived and resolved it, writing that this matter is simple among our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: "Hazal"</note>: that the beginning was with a quantity of twenty-three or forty coins, as mentioned by the legal decisor <note>original: "Posek" - a rabbi who issues a binding ruling on Jewish law</note>. ->From the beginning to the end<-

To this, one must respond according to the appearance of things: that this was on the part of the sellers, who could not fully resolve their intentions in their hearts. Every person knows that regarding those five hundred pieces of silver for which he sold them the second time, he already held a legal presumption of ownership <note>original: "hazakah"</note> from the first transaction. Everything follows what was sold to him for the majority of the money or at an equivalent price. It was never the seller's intention that because of this detail the sale should be canceled; rather, the seller bears all the legal responsibility and obligation, and the buyer has only to pay what remains. 
The pious Rabbi, Rabbi Isaac, wrote: 
"We have never seen a person seek to grant ownership of money to his fellow..."

And peace be upon Israel.

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>While the previous page focused on the literal meaning of the commandment to love God as taught by Moses, this page transitions into the "Wisdom of Truth" (Kabbalah). It shifts from the "plain meaning" of the text to a technical explanation of how Divine influence is channeled through various spiritual structures and how man's character can mirror the Divine.</meta>

-> With the Help of Heaven <-

Regarding the matter: all the **Rectifications** <note>Hebrew: *Tikkunim*; refers to the spiritual arrangements that allow Divine light to enter the world without overwhelming it</note> are parts of the **Divine Persona** <note>Hebrew: *Partzuf*; a configuration of Divine attributes (Sefirot) that functions as a single unit</note>, the half-partition, and all the power planted within them. By the power of the most ancient Rectification of all, which is the **Prime Mover**, for there is no light that shines without a mediating agent that draws it down. This matter is similar to the analogy of a rope that pulls the action—effecting change from a distance—yet we have heard [the phrase] "every hand of man," etc. <note>original: "kol yad adam." This refers to the principle that human action in the physical world triggers spiritual movements above.</note> And if any trace <note>Hebrew: *Reshimu*; the impression or "scent" of light left behind after the light has departed</note> of the Divine Abundance is awakened, that Rectification will draw it down, and the influence will come to the recipient, and so forth.

And this power is not complete except through the union of the **Names MaH and BaN** <note>These are specific numerical expansions of the four-letter Name of God, representing different spiritual energies</note>, and through this, **Father and Mother** <note>Hebrew: *Abba v'Imma*; the creative archetypes of Wisdom and Understanding</note> unite completely. For from their external aspect, the "External Forces" <note>Hebrew: *Chitzonim*; often refers to the forces of "the other side" or impurity that draw sustenance from the outer edges of holiness</note> are drawn <gloss>as is known</gloss>, as is the radiance of the external lights that shine, and so forth, from level to level.

Behold, the **Surrounding Light** <note>Hebrew: *Or Makif*; a pervasive Divine light that is too intense to be internalized</note> of one Rectification is considered the **Inner Light** <note>Hebrew: *Or Pnimiy*; light that is tailored to and contained within a spiritual vessel</note> of another, and vice versa. Indeed, the Surrounding Rectification, which is greater than it, is viewed in terms of the "Inner" [of the level above]. It appears there is no difference in essence, except that the level clothed within its companion is called **Inner** to it, and that which clothes it is called **Surrounding**. And if the clothed level were to go outside, it would also be called Surrounding.

And similarly, in terms of "Innerness," there is nothing but the **Divine Essence** alone, which is found within everything. And that which Surrounds all is also one thing without division; it is only from the perspective of the **Vessels** <note>Hebrew: *Kelim*; the structures that "hold" and define the Divine light</note> that these distinctions are impressed. Because the Vessels are those Rectifications we mentioned—which receive power from the Inner aspect to perform their action—it is relative to them that we perceive Surrounding and Inner lights.

Every Rectification has two levels: Inner and Surrounding. The radiance of its Inner aspect is what sustains and vitalizes it, for no created being has standing or vitality without the **Divine Will** that is within it. And the radiance of its Surrounding aspect is that which comes to it from the **Supernal Light** that guides it. All these things are according to our capacity, to answer some of our perceptions and to explain the immense secrets within the **System of Emanation** <note>Hebrew: *Atzilut*; the highest of the four spiritual worlds, where the Divine light is most apparent</note>. Every Rectification is called "Light," which is the Divine Will restricted within the receiving Vessel, and from it, sustenance is drawn to all creatures according to their nature.

All these names of the seven levels accepted by all the Sages are only perspectives to understand a little, from the standpoint of what has been "impressed" upon us, to gain wisdom regarding the Creator (blessed be He) and to resemble Him in our character traits. For as our Sages of blessed memory said: "Just as He is merciful, so you should be merciful <insert>and gracious</insert>," etc. This is the inner intention of the **Wisdom of Truth** <note>Hebrew: *Chochmat HaEmet*; a traditional name for Kabbalah</note>: to find the straight path to serve the Creator (blessed be He) with a whole heart, according to His will. And may all be rectified in the **Rectification of the World** <note>Hebrew: *Tikkun Olam*</note> under the Kingdom of the Almighty, speedily in our days, Amen, may it be His will.

<unclear>May his grace stand for us</unclear> <note>original: "Chen YKMOV" - Likely a scribal signature or acronym involving the word "Grace/Hidden Wisdom" (Chen).</note>

**Vocabulary:** Rectifications, Divine Persona, Names MaH and BaN, Father and Mother, Inner Light, Surrounding Light, Divine Essence, Emanation, Wisdom of Truth (Kabbalah), Divine Will.

It concludes that the purpose of this mystical study is to allow humans to emulate God's mercy and serve Him with a "whole heart."</summary>

<keywords>Tikkunim, Partzuf, Inner and Surrounding Light, Divine Essence, Atzilut, Kabbalah, Divine Will, Emulating God, Mercy, MaH and BaN</keywords>

A story <unclear>of</unclear> a Jew who <unclear>lived</unclear> in <unclear>Kraków</unclear> <margin>of blessed memory</margin>. This same man had a son, and the son was a very great scholar <term>lamdan: a person well-versed in Talmudic study</term> in the Torah. He studied with a Rabbi, and he had a great desire to learn much Torah, as he was told he was a great scholar. Because of this, he was given a large dowry <note>original: "nadan"</note> <unclear>according to</unclear> <unclear>his worth</unclear> of 3,000 Reichsthaler <note>A common European silver currency of the period</note>, as he was intended to marry <insert>a rich man's daughter</insert>. <unclear>But</unclear> the young man <margin>at that time</margin> did not want to <unclear>marry</unclear>; <unclear>rather</unclear>, he wanted to learn much Torah. He said, "Why should I need a wife and children? <margin>It is</margin> better that I should study Torah for its own sake <term>lishmah: the ideal of studying Torah for the sake of the divine commandment itself rather than for status or reward</term>." ->In the name of the Exalted One, Blessed be He, I have set [the Lord always before me]<-

---

And peace. Hereby, it is required that a person should prepare himself with his heart beforehand, when he wishes to approach the Torah of the Lord. When he applies himself to it, he should do so with great strength and awe, for which you must give your blood and your wealth <note>original: "damim u-ma'od," echoing the Shema prayer's command to love God with all one's resources</note> with all your power. Upon this [foundation], you shall study the Five Books of Moses <term>Chamisha Chumshei Torah: The Pentateuch</term>, and one should repeat the Five Books of Moses with great strength and joy during the time of study.

And he should have his joy like one who stands upon the shore of the sea, which is never still, fearing lest it swallow him; thus he is in awe and afraid. So too is the law: that he should walk with the fear of heaven <term>Yirat Shamayim: Awe and reverence for the Divine</term>, and every single moment he should fear lest he sin, lest <term>Hagehinnom</term> <gloss>Hell</gloss> swallow him. 
<margin>Psalm 119</margin> Through this study, he will be considered truly pious <note>original: "hasid"</note>. And concerning this it is said: "Be wise; happy is the man who lives at all times meditating on the Five Books of Moses." Beside him, he should dedicate his time at every moment and every season, and he should always serve the Lord, may He be blessed, continually. Your fear [of God] should always be upon your head at every moment and every hour, and you should not divert your mind from the attribute of fear and the might of the Lord. These are the matters of the Torah: to be in true dread and awe, etc. A man who holds fast to the Torah, his heart will always be trembling. 

I have heard in the name of the Holy One, Blessed be He: we see that when a man is truly in the state of a servant, so too it is incumbent upon the man [to act]; and afterward, let him perform the deeds of the pious <note>original: "hasid hasiduta"</note>. And who is like Your people Israel, who are cleaved to the Holy One, Blessed be He, and the Divine Presence <note>original: "Shekhinah"</note> is always with them, upon the holy man <note>original: "ha-kadosh"</note>.

---

And so it is written in the Jerusalem Talmud <note>original: "Yerushalmi"</note> in the chapter of Hananiah, and these are its words: "And let the fear of the Lord be upon you always, at every moment and every hour, and Torah and good deeds always." And so let it be in your heart: "God forbid <note>original: "halila"</note> that I should sin against this law." And do not turn from it to the right or the left, and fulfill that which is written:
> Before me is the Lord, before me is the Lord, before me is the Lord, before me is the Lord, etc.
I have set <note>original: "shiviti"</note> the Lord always before me. Let His fame and His memory be always in your mouth and at all times. Let the work of your hands be according to the word of the Lord, may He be blessed; therefore, let your business be conducted properly. He should be in the Torah always, and so too the law: that he will fear Him, to bring upon them the fear of heaven. He shall be a great pious man through his study of these matters. And the meaning of the aforementioned subject is:
> I have set the Lord always before me.
The Name, may He be blessed, shall be before his eyes in heart and soul, and in your heart shall come great trembling and dread—the word of the Lord regarding the aforementioned verse.
